id: architecture-angular-high-frequency-ui
title: Angular interface with many changing values
topic: architecture
summary: Show many values that change often, with zoneless signals, OnPush, few DOM nodes and measures first. Angular 22.
tool: angular
version: "22"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4]

# Angular interface with many changing values

Contents: 1. What refreshes · 2. Measured case · 3. Rules for a synoptic · Sources

## 1. What refreshes the screen (zoneless and OnPush)

- Zoneless is the default since Angular 21. A refresh comes from a signal read by a template, `markForCheck()` (the `async` pipe calls it), `ComponentRef.setInput`, or a template event listener. [S1]
- OnPush is the default for new applications in v22; `ChangeDetectionStrategy.Default` becomes `Eager`. [S4]
- An OnPush component is checked again only on a new input reference, an event in its subtree, a mark, or a signal it reads; then only the components that read the signal refresh. Changing an object in place triggers nothing. [S2]
- In zoneless mode, a message from the server updates the screen only if it writes a signal or marks the component: every value shown on a synoptic goes through a signal.

## 2. A measured case (large table)

| Change | Effect measured by the author |
| --- | --- |
| OnPush, row and cell components, `@for` with `track` | Script of one click: 612 ms to 9 ms |
| Fewer DOM nodes (the cell becomes the component) | 513,000 to 221,000 nodes; one click rendered under 100 ms |
| Live updates every 50 ms, after these changes | Handled in about 7 ms |
| Heavy computation in a Web Worker, cache | First display from 3 s to 0.3 s |
| Virtual scrolling | Not used; kept as a last resort |

A table of about 73,000 cells, not a synoptic; Angular version not given; April 2025; single source. [S3]

## 3. Rules for a synoptic view (Angular)

- One component per symbol, one signal per shown value, the fewest DOM nodes per symbol.
- Batch the server messages and write the signals once per batch (`docs/architecture/real-time-push.md`).
- Measure before you optimize; virtual scrolling only after a measure shows the need.

## Sources

- [S1] Angular, "Angular without ZoneJS (Zoneless)", <https://angular.dev/guide/zoneless>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] Angular, "Skipping component subtrees", <https://angular.dev/best-practices/skipping-subtrees>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S3] Vincent Bourdeix, "Boost Angular Performance: Real-World Optimization Case Study", DEV Community, 2025-04-28, <https://dev.to/vbourdeix/boost-angular-performance-real-world-optimization-case-study-58c7>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] Angular, "Announcing Angular v22", 2026-06-03, <https://blog.angular.dev/announcing-angular-v22-c52bb83a4664>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: architecture-api-contract-openapi
title: API contract with OpenAPI
topic: architecture

# API contract with OpenAPI

Contents: 1. Rule · 2. Server side · 3. Angular client · 4. Versions and contracts · Sources

## 1. Rule of the API contract

The OpenAPI file generated at build is the contract. The Angular client is generated from it, never written by hand. A diff of the file shows every contract change in review.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  A["API code (.NET)"] --> B["Build: generated OpenAPI document"]
  B --> C["OpenAPI file versioned in the repository"]
  C --> D["TypeScript client generator"]
  D --> E["Angular client library"]
  E --> F["Angular build: a broken contract fails compilation"]
```

## 2. Server side (ASP.NET Core)

| Fact | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| Swashbuckle left the templates in .NET 9 | `Microsoft.AspNetCore.OpenApi` generates the document [S1] |
| Two calls | `AddOpenApi()` and `MapOpenApi()`; route `/openapi/{documentName}.json`; a test UI is added apart [S2] |
| Document version | 3.1 by default in .NET 10, 3.2 in .NET 11; set with `OpenApiVersion` [S2] [S3] |
| Build-time generation | Package `Microsoft.Extensions.ApiDescription.Server`; folder set with `OpenApiDocumentsDirectory`; it runs the entry point, so startup code runs [S2] |
| Endpoint not protected | No authorization by default; examples map it in development only [S2] |
| Minimal APIs | Recommended for new projects; controllers for extensible model binding, advanced validation or OData [S4] |
| Validation | `AddValidation()` checks query, headers and body and returns 400 (.NET 10) [S3] |
| Errors | `AddProblemDetails()`, `UseExceptionHandler()`, `UseStatusCodePages()`; `IExceptionHandler` since ASP.NET Core 8 [S5] |
| Cookie authentication | API endpoints get 401 or 403 instead of a redirect in .NET 10 [S3] |

## 3. Angular client (generated)

| Tool | State on its page | Known limits |
| --- | --- | --- |
| openapi-generator `typescript-angular` | Stable; Angular 9 to 22; option `withHttpResource` | `oneOf`, `anyOf`, `allOf` and unions listed as unsupported [S6] |
| NSwag | MIT; Angular template | Page names Swagger 2.0 and OpenAPI 3.0 only [S7] |
| Orval, Hey API, Kiota | Other generators | Not compared here |

.NET 10 emits OpenAPI 3.1 and .NET 11 describes C# 15 unions with `anyOf`, which the generator above does not list: set `OpenApiVersion` to 3.0, or keep unions out of API contracts until the generator handles them. [S2] [S3] [S6]

## 4. Versions and contracts (API)

- Version an API with `Asp.Versioning.Http` (minimal APIs) or `Asp.Versioning.Mvc`; MIT, .NET Foundation. [S8]
- A service update must not break its clients: design for compatibility. [S9]
- Pact tests a consumer-driven contract, born from the consumer tests; it fits between teams that deliver apart. In one repository, the generated client and the OpenAPI diff give the essential. [S10]

## Sources

- [S1] dotnet/aspnetcore, "Announcement: Swashbuckle.AspNetCore is being removed in .NET 9", issue 54599, <https://github.com/dotnet/aspnetcore/issues/54599>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Microsoft, "Generate OpenAPI documents", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/openapi/aspnetcore-openapi>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S3] Microsoft, "What's new in ASP.NET Core in .NET 10", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/release-notes/aspnetcore-10.0>, and "Announcing .NET 11", 2026-11-10, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S4] Microsoft, "APIs overview", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/apis>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S5] Microsoft, "Handle errors in ASP.NET Core APIs", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/error-handling-api>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S6] OpenAPI Generator, "Documentation for the typescript-angular Generator", <https://openapi-generator.tech/docs/generators/typescript-angular>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S7] Rico Suter and contributors, "NSwag", README, <https://github.com/RicoSuter/NSwag>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S8] dotnet, "aspnet-api-versioning", README, <https://github.com/dotnet/aspnet-api-versioning>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S9] Microsoft, "Microservices architecture style", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/guide/architecture-styles/microservices>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S10] Pact Foundation, "Introduction", <https://docs.pact.io/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# Modular monolith or microservices

Contents: 1. Rule · 2. Decision · 3. Module rules · 4. Anti-patterns · Sources

## 1. Rule of decomposition (start with a modular monolith)

- Start with a modular monolith. Split a service only on a need that is seen, never by anticipation.
- Successful microservices mostly start from a monolith grown too big; systems built as microservices from the start often had serious trouble. [S1]
- A monolith suits a small team; it gets in the way when many teams change one code base and deploy in a queue. [S3]
- Four documented moves back from microservices (Istio, Prime Video, Segment, InVision) cite cost in all four cases. [S4]
- Opposite view: for a large system whose domain is well known, independent subsystems from the start can be better. [S2]

## 2. Decision (split a service or not)

Read from top to bottom: each diamond is a question to ask before a separate service.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  A["New application or rework"] --> B{"Several autonomous teams must deliver apart?"}
  B -- no --> C{"Must one part scale or fail alone?"}
  B -- yes --> D{"Are the domain boundaries known and stable?"}
  C -- no --> M["Modular monolith: one deployable, one module per domain"]
  C -- yes --> E["Extract that one module as a service"]
  D -- no --> M
  D -- yes --> S["Services per domain, private data, versioned contracts"]
  M --> R["Review at each need seen, never by anticipation"]
```

## 3. Module rules (modular monolith)

- Each module owns its data (its own schema or database), exposes an explicit public interface, and never reads the tables of another module; without these rules it is a plain monolith. [S7]
- When modules talk by events through an outbox, only the transport changes when one is extracted later. [S7] [S9]
- Service boundaries follow business capabilities, not technical layers; domain analysis and bounded contexts find them. [S6] [S5]
- Microservices cost complexity, testing, network latency, data integrity without transactions, operations, versioning and skills. [S5]
- The guide ".NET Microservices: Architecture for Containerized .NET Applications" stays at .NET 7 and its eShopOnContainers is no production model; the current reference app is `dotnet/eShop`, on .NET 10 with Aspire. [S8]

## 4. Anti-patterns of decomposition

- Splitting without knowing the domain; database entities used as events; shared common libraries between services; services exposed without a gateway. [S5]
- Microservices for one small team. [S1] [S3]

## Sources

- [S1] Martin Fowler, "Monolith First", 2015-06-03, <https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/MonolithFirst.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Stefan Tilkov, "Don't start with a monolith", 2015-06-09, <https://martinfowler.com/articles/dont-start-monolith.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] Chris Richardson, "When to use the microservice architecture: part 5", microservices.io, 2021, <https://microservices.io/post/microservices/2021/02/14/why-microservices-part-5-monolith.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] Su, Li, Taibi, "From Microservice to Monolith: A Multivocal Literature Review", Electronics 13(8), 2024, <https://mdpi.com/2079-9292/13/8/1452>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY, summarized.
- [S5] Microsoft, "Microservices architecture style", Azure Architecture Center, updated 2025-07-11, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/guide/architecture-styles/microservices>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S6] Microsoft, "Use domain analysis to model microservices", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/microservices/model/domain-analysis>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S7] Milan Jovanović, "Modular Monolith vs Microservices: How to Choose", 2026-07-14, <https://milanjovanovic.tech/blog/modular-monolith-vs-microservices.md>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S8] Microsoft, ".NET Microservices: Architecture for Containerized .NET Applications", v7.0, and dotnet, "eShop", <https://github.com/dotnet/eShop>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S9] Chris Richardson, "Pattern: Transactional outbox", <https://microservices.io/patterns/data/transactional-outbox.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# Code review checklist

Contents: A. Every change · B. C# and .NET · C. TypeScript and Angular · Sources

What the lint and the build check is not reviewed again: run the check commands of `AGENTS.md` first. A personal preference is marked as optional and never blocks. [S1]

## A. Every change (code review)

- Design: does the change belong here, and does it fit the rest of the system?
- Function: does it do what the author meant, also on edge cases and on errors?
- Complexity: can a reader understand it quickly? Is any code made general for a supposed future need?
- Tests: are there tests that would fail if the code were broken?
- Names: does every name say what it holds or does?
- Comments: do comments say why, not what?
- Consistency: does it follow the conventions of the project and of this base?
- Documentation: are the README, the API contract or the base updated when behavior changes?
- Every line: did you read every line you are asked to review, and the context around it?
- Good things: say what is well done. [S1]

## B. C# and .NET (code review)

- No singleton keeps a scoped service (captive dependency). [S2]
- Options are bound, validated and checked at start; no `IOptionsSnapshot<T>` in a singleton. [S2]
- One resilience handler per HTTP client; no retry of a POST without disabling unsafe methods. [S2]
- Log calls use message templates with named fields, never string interpolation. [S2]
- One short `DbContext` per unit of work; never two operations at once on one context; every async call awaited. [S2]
- No library of `docs/dotnet/paid-packages.md` added without the user's agreement.
- No pattern of `docs/architecture/outdated-libraries-and-patterns.md` for the target framework.

## C. TypeScript and Angular (code review)

- Every value shown comes from a signal; no object changed in place under OnPush. [S2]
- No effect used to copy a state into another: derive it with `computed`. [S2]
- Server reads through `httpResource` or a service; writes through `HttpClient`. [S2]
- No subscription left open after the component ends.
- Folders by feature; no new NgModule; no old template syntax.
- Symbols of a synoptic follow `docs/synoptics/`; property names follow the naming document.

## Sources

- [S1] Google, "What to look for in a code review", Google Engineering Practices, <https://google.github.io/eng-practices/review/reviewer/looking-for.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] The documents of this topic (`dotnet-service-patterns.md`, `angular-app-structure.md`, `angular-high-frequency-ui.md`) and their sources, 2026-11-13.

# .NET service patterns

Contents: 1. Do and do not · 2. Resilience and Polly · 3. Aspire · Sources

## 1. Do and do not (.NET service)

| Subject | Do | Do not |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dependency injection | Thread-safe singletons; scope validation on; fast synchronous factories | Captive dependency (a singleton that keeps a scoped service); service locator; `BuildServiceProvider` while configuring; `Task.Result` in a factory [S1] |
| Options | `AddOptions<T>().Bind(...)`, `ValidateDataAnnotations()`, `ValidateOnStart()`; `IOptionsMonitor<T>` in a singleton | `IOptionsSnapshot<T>` in a singleton [S2] |
| HTTP resilience | `AddStandardResilienceHandler()`: rate limiter, 30 s total timeout, 3 retries, circuit breaker, 10 s per attempt | Several handlers stacked; retrying a POST without `DisableForUnsafeHttpMethods()` [S3] |
| Logging | Message templates with named fields; `[LoggerMessage]` on hot paths | String interpolation in a log call [S5] |
| Telemetry | `ILogger` for logs, `Meter` for metrics, `ActivitySource` for traces; OTLP export | — [S4] |
| Health | Readiness probe with the tagged checks; liveness probe without dependency checks | A heavy query in a probe [S6] |
| Data access | One short `DbContext` per unit of work; `IDbContextFactory<T>` outside an HTTP request; await every async call | Two operations in parallel on one context; the in-memory provider for tests [S7] [S8] |
| Data tests | Test against the real engine (a container started by the tests); a repository as test double, knowing its cost | Mocking `DbSet` for queries [S7] |

- A `DbContext` is not thread-safe; the typical error starts with "A second operation started on this context". [S8]
- The EF Core in-memory provider is "strongly discouraged" as a fake engine. [S7]

## 2. Resilience and Polly (.NET)

- `Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Resilience` builds on Polly. [S3]
- The old package `Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Polly` is deprecated by the `Polly.Extensions.Http` repository, archived on 2023-11-13. [S9]
- Polly adopts a maintenance fee for the next major version after 2026-11-16, on direct references only; going through `Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Resilience` is not subject to it. [S10]
- Rule: resilience through the `Microsoft.Extensions.*` packages; no direct reference to Polly without a recorded decision.

## 3. Aspire (local orchestration)

- Aspire (MIT) describes, runs, debugs and deploys a distributed application; its core, the AppHost, describes the architecture in code. [S11]
- It replaces neither Kubernetes nor a runtime: the AppHost runs at local start and in the deployment pipeline, then stops; never deploy it as a service. [S11]
- `aspire publish` writes Helm charts and `aspire deploy` installs them in the current `kubectl` context (Helm 4.2.0 or later). [S11]
- The "ServiceDefaults" project of the templates sets OpenTelemetry, health endpoints and HTTP resilience; it works without the Aspire orchestration. [S4]

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft, "Dependency injection guidelines", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/extensions/dependency-injection-guidelines>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] Microsoft, "Options pattern in .NET", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/extensions/options>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S3] Microsoft, "Build resilient HTTP apps: Key development patterns", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/resilience/http-resilience>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S4] Microsoft, ".NET observability with OpenTelemetry", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/diagnostics/observability-with-otel>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S5] Microsoft, "Compile-time logging source generation", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/extensions/logger-message-generator>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S6] Microsoft, "Health checks in ASP.NET Core", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/host-and-deploy/health-checks>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S7] Microsoft, "Choosing a testing strategy" (EF Core), <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/ef/core/testing/choosing-a-testing-strategy>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S8] Microsoft, "DbContext Lifetime, Configuration, and Initialization", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/ef/core/dbcontext-configuration/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S9] App-vNext, "Polly.Extensions.Http", archived repository, <https://github.com/App-vNext/Polly.Extensions.Http>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S10] The Polly Project, "Introducing the Open Source Maintenance Fee for Polly", 2026-07-14, updated 2026-10-12, <https://thepollyproject.org/2026/07/14/polly-osmf-announcement.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S11] Aspire, "Frequently asked questions" and "Deploy to Kubernetes", <https://aspire.dev/get-started/faq/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# Messaging between services

Contents: 1. When · 2. Outbox and idempotence · 3. Broker · 4. Libraries and licenses · Sources

## 1. When to use a queue (messaging)

- Between services, asynchronous messaging avoids coupling and chatty call chains. [S1]
- Inside a modular monolith, a direct call is enough until you need to decouple in time: long work, recovery after a failure, several subscribers. A queue never replaces a method call.
- The base sets no bus library by default: the choice is made per project, with its license.

## 2. Transactional outbox and idempotent consumers

- Updating the database and publishing a message atomically needs a transactional outbox: the message is written to a table in the same transaction, and a relay publishes it later. [S2]
- The relay can publish twice: every consumer must be idempotent (store the ids of the messages already handled). [S2]
- CQRS with two databases syncs the read side by events, with the same care. [S3]

## 3. Broker (RabbitMQ or Kafka)

The RabbitMQ team writes that "RabbitMQ for queues, Kafka for streams" is outdated: RabbitMQ has replayable streams since 3.9 (2021), Kafka has queues since 4.2 (2026); its advice is to start with RabbitMQ and add Kafka for what only Kafka does (log compaction, tiered storage, Kafka Streams). [S4] Interested single source.

## 4. .NET bus libraries and their licenses (checked 2026-11-12)

| Library | Model | Free use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| MassTransit 9 and later | Commercial license, key required | 100 % discount under one million dollars of revenue and for non-profits; v8 keeps its open license, support until 2026-12-31 [S5] |
| NServiceBus | Commercial: free in development, paid in production | Community tier: 3 endpoints, 10,000 messages per day [S6] |
| Wolverine | MIT; durable inbox and outbox | Paid support offers apart [S7] |
| Rebus | MIT | Commercial module apart (Rebus Pro) [S8] |

Never propose a library of this table without its license and version; if the project already uses it, report it as a risk to decide. MediatR is an in-memory mediator, not a bus: see `docs/dotnet/paid-packages.md`.

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft, "Microservices architecture style", Azure Architecture Center, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/guide/architecture-styles/microservices>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Chris Richardson, "Pattern: Transactional outbox", <https://microservices.io/patterns/data/transactional-outbox.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] Microsoft, "CQRS pattern", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/patterns/cqrs>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] RabbitMQ, "RabbitMQ vs. Apache Kafka", documentation 4.3, <https://rabbitmq.com/docs/compare/kafka>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S5] Massient, "Massient", MassTransit licenses, <https://massient.com/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S6] Particular Software, "Licensing", <https://particular.net/licensing>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S7] Wolverine, home page, <https://wolverinefx.net/>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S8] rebus-org, "Rebus", README, <https://github.com/rebus-org/Rebus>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.

# Outdated libraries and patterns

Contents: 1. Rule · 2. Retired .NET patterns · 3. Retired Angular patterns · 4. Over-engineering · Sources

## 1. Rule for outdated libraries

Never propose a library that changed license (MediatR, AutoMapper, MassTransit, FluentAssertions, Polly as a direct reference) without its license status and the version concerned; if the project already uses it, report it as a risk to decide. The table and the free replacements are in `docs/dotnet/paid-packages.md`. A tracking site lists more packages (Moq, IdentityServer, ImageSharp, QuestPDF, EPPlus and others); single secondary source. [S1]

## 2. Retired .NET patterns

| Old habit | State | Write instead |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `AddSwaggerGen()` and Swashbuckle by default | Out of the templates in .NET 9 | `AddOpenApi()`, `MapOpenApi()` [S2] [S3] |
| `Microsoft.Extensions.Http.Polly`, `AddPolicyHandler` | Deprecated by the Polly project | `AddStandardResilienceHandler()` [S4] |
| EF Core in-memory provider for tests | Strongly discouraged | Real engine in a container, or a repository double [S5] |
| Controllers by default for a new API | Minimal APIs recommended | `MapGet`, `TypedResults` [S3] |
| eShopOnContainers as a model | Guide stuck at .NET 7, app replaced | `dotnet/eShop` as a reference to read [S3] |

## 3. Retired Angular patterns

| Old habit | State | Write instead |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NgModules, `standalone: true` by hand | Standalone by default | A standalone component without the flag [S6] |
| `*ngIf`, `*ngFor`, `*ngSwitch` | Deprecated in v20 | `@if`, `@for` with `track`, `@switch` [S7] [S6] |
| Constructor injection, `@Input()`, `@Output()` | Replaced in the guide | `inject()`, `input()`, `output()`, `model()` [S6] |
| `ngClass`, `ngStyle`, `@HostBinding`, `@HostListener` | Discouraged | `class` and `style` bindings, the `host` object [S6] |
| zone.js, `NgZone.onStable` | Zoneless by default in v21 | Signals, `afterNextRender` [S8] |
| `changeDetection: OnPush` written everywhere | OnPush by default in v22 | Nothing to write [S9] |
| Karma and Jasmine for a new project | Vitest by default in v21 | Vitest [S7] |
| `@Injectable({ providedIn: 'root' })` | Replaceable in most cases in v22 | `@Service` [S9] |
| Reactive forms for a new form | Signal Forms stable in v22 | Signal Forms [S9] |
| Suffixes `.component.ts`, `.service.ts` | Dropped by the 2025 style guide for new projects | `user-profile.ts` [S6] |

## 4. Signs of over-engineering

| Sign | Why it is a signal |
| --- | --- |
| Microservices for one small team | The premium only pays back on a complex system |
| Four projects, CQRS pipeline and domain events for five CRUD endpoints | Main risk of clean architecture |
| A service and a repository that only pass calls through | Abstractions that should not exist |
| A message bus between modules of one deployable without a need to decouple | Operations cost and duplicate messages |
| A global store for a local state | Signals and a service are enough |
| Virtual scrolling before any measure | Last resort |
| Code made general for a supposed future need | A classic point of code review |

These signs come from `choose-decomposition.md`, `structure-dotnet-solution.md`, `messaging.md` and `angular-high-frequency-ui.md` of this topic.

## Sources

- [S1] Dariusz Woźniak, "FOSSed", <https://dariusz-wozniak.github.io/fossed/>, read 2026-11-12, secondary source, summarized.

id: architecture-real-time-push
title: Real-time push to the browser
topic: architecture
summary: SignalR, Server-Sent Events or gRPC, deployment on several replicas, message format and defaults. ASP.NET Core 10.
tool: dotnet
version: "10"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6, S7, S8]

---

# Real-time push to the browser

Contents: 1. Choice · 2. SignalR on several replicas · 3. Format and defaults · 4. Path of a value · Sources

## 1. Choice of transport (SignalR, SSE, gRPC)

| Criterion | SignalR | Server-Sent Events |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Direction | Both ways, hub method calls [S1] | Server to client only [S2] |
| Transport | WebSocket, then SSE, then long polling [S1] | HTTP, UTF-8 text [S2] |
| Broadcast and groups | Built in [S1] | To write |
| Format | JSON or MessagePack [S1] [S4] | Text |
| Reconnect | Stateful reconnect option since .NET 8 [S6] | Automatic in the browser [S2] |
| Limit | Session affinity on several servers [S3] | Without HTTP/2, 6 connections per browser and domain, all tabs together [S2] |
| .NET | `AddSignalR()`, `MapHub<T>()` | `TypedResults.ServerSentEvents(...)` since .NET 10 [S8] |

- Dashboards, monitoring and high-frequency updates are good candidates for SignalR. [S1]
- A browser cannot call gRPC directly: gRPC-Web or JSON transcoding; gRPC-Web supports only unary calls and server streaming from a browser. Keep gRPC between services. [S7]

## 2. SignalR on several replicas (scale out)

- Every request of one connection must reach the same server: session affinity is required. [S3]
- Exception: all clients use WebSocket only, with `skipNegotiation` on the client. [S3]
- The Redis backplane is the advised way on your own infrastructure; it keeps the affinity requirement, except for the case above. [S3]
- On Kubernetes, set affinity at the cluster entry, or use WebSocket only (`docs/kubernetes/ingress-gateway.md`).

## 3. Format and defaults (SignalR)

- MessagePack makes smaller messages than JSON, with unreadable traces; pitfalls: C# casing of property names on the client, `DateTime.Kind` not kept, strict types, `UntrustedData` option advised. [S4]
- For a continuous flow, a hub method that returns `IAsyncEnumerable<T>`; the client cancels by disposing its subscription. [S5]
- Defaults: incoming message limited to 32 KB; ping every 15 s; client lost after 30 s; 64 KB buffers before backpressure; detailed errors off. [S6]

## 4. Path of a value to a synoptic (SignalR and Angular)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  S["Simulation service"] --> H["SignalR hub: one group per synoptic view"]
  H --> W["WebSocket"]
  W --> C["Angular service: batches the messages received"]
  C --> G["Signals: one per shown value"]
  G --> V["OnPush components: only those that read the signal refresh"]
```

No public source gives a measured rate for synoptics. Send batches of changes at a fixed rate rather than one message per variable; subscribe a client only to the views it shows; switch to MessagePack if message size matters; measure.

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft, "Overview of ASP.NET Core SignalR", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/signalr/introduction>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] Mozilla, "Using server-sent events", MDN, <https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Server-sent_events/Using_server-sent_events>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA, summarized in own words.
- [S3] Microsoft, "ASP.NET Core SignalR hosting and scaling", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/signalr/scale>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S4] Microsoft, "Use MessagePack Hub Protocol in SignalR for ASP.NET Core", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/signalr/messagepackhubprotocol>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S5] Microsoft, "Use streaming in ASP.NET Core SignalR", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/signalr/streaming>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S6] Microsoft, "ASP.NET Core SignalR configuration", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/signalr/configuration>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S7] Microsoft, "Compare gRPC services with HTTP APIs" and "gRPC-Web in ASP.NET Core gRPC apps", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/grpc/comparison>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S8] Microsoft, "What's new in ASP.NET Core in .NET 10", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/release-notes/aspnetcore-10.0>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.

id: architecture-review-checklist
title: Architecture review checklist
topic: architecture
summary: Closed questions of an architecture review, by theme, each answered yes, no or unknown, with the themes to keep.
tool: none
version: "none"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1]
license: MIT (written for this base)

# Architecture review checklist

Contents: A. Goals · B. Decomposition · C. .NET structure · D. API contract · E. Real-time · F. Messaging · G. .NET services · H. Data · I. Angular · J. Live display · K. Libraries · Sources
Answer each question with yes, no or unknown. A "no" is a finding. An "unknown" is a question for the user.

## A. Goals and decisions (always)

- Are the quality goals written and ranked?
- Is there a record of the main decisions, each with its reason and the rejected alternative?
- Does each deployable unit, layer and library have a stated reason?

## B. Decomposition (when there is more than one deployable unit, or a split is proposed)

- Does each service exist for a stated need: a team that releases alone, a load to scale alone, a failure to isolate?
- Are the boundaries drawn around business capabilities, not technical layers?
- Does each service or module own its data? No table is read by two of them.
- Can each service be released without releasing another one the same day?
- Is one business operation done without a long chain of calls between services?
- If the team is small and the services are released together: is a modular monolith ruled out for a stated reason?

## C. .NET solution structure (when there is .NET code)

- Is the code organized by feature or module, with a public interface per module?
- Does each layer do work? No service or repository only forwards calls.
- Is CQRS used only where reads and writes differ? Not on simple CRUD.
- Are commands and queries plain classes called directly, with no mediator library required?
- Is there a test that enforces the dependency rules between modules or layers?

## D. API contract (when Angular calls a .NET API)

- Is the OpenAPI document generated by the built-in ASP.NET Core package, at build time?
- Is the generated document committed, so that a contract change shows in a diff?
- Is the Angular client generated from that document, not written by hand?
- Does the client generator support every schema the API emits? Check the OpenAPI version, `oneOf` and `anyOf`.
- Is the API version explicit, and are breaking changes shipped as a new version?
- Do errors use the problem details format, and is request validation enabled?
- Is the OpenAPI endpoint closed or protected outside development?

## E. Real-time (when the server pushes data to the browser)

- Is the transport chosen for the need: SignalR for two-way or groups, server-sent events for a simple one-way stream? gRPC is not called from the browser.
- With more than one replica: is session affinity set, or do all clients use WebSockets only with negotiation skipped?
- With more than one replica: is there a backplane?
- Are clients subscribed only to the views they display, through groups?
- Are changes sent in batches at a fixed rate, not one message per value?
- Is each message under the receive size limit? Is MessagePack considered when size matters?
- Are detailed errors off in production?
- Was the message rate measured against the goal?

## F. Messaging (when a queue or a bus is used or proposed)

- Is there a stated need to decouple in time: long work, retry after failure, several subscribers?
- Is a message written in the same transaction as the data, through an outbox?
- Are consumers idempotent?
- Is the license of the messaging library checked for its exact version?

## G. .NET service patterns (when there is .NET code)

- Does no singleton hold a scoped service?
- Are options bound, validated and checked at start?
- Is there exactly one resilience handler per HTTP client, with retries off for unsafe methods?
- Do log messages use named placeholders?
- Are logs, metrics and traces exported with OpenTelemetry?
- Are readiness and liveness checks separate? The liveness check calls no dependency.

## H. Data access (when a database is used)

- Is each database context short-lived, one per unit of work?
- Is no context used by two operations at the same time?
- Do data tests run against the real database engine?

## I. Angular structure and state (when there is Angular code)

- Are folders organized by feature, not by type?
- Are components standalone, with lazy-loaded feature routes?
- Is local state in signals, and derived state in `computed`?
- Is shared state in a service with signals? A store library is used only for a stated need.
- Are server reads done with `httpResource` or a service, and writes with `HttpClient`?
- With several apps or libraries: is there a lint rule that enforces module boundaries?

## J. Angular live display (when the screen shows values that change often)

- Does each displayed value come from a signal?
- Is each symbol a small component, with as few DOM nodes as possible?
- Are lists rendered with `@for` and `track`?
- Are incoming messages grouped before they update signals?
- Is heavy computation moved off the main thread?
- Was the display measured before any further optimization?

## K. Libraries and licenses (always)

- Is each library checked in `outdated-libraries-and-patterns.md`?
- For a library with a paid or restricted license: is the version pinned and the decision recorded?
- Does the code use a pattern listed as outdated for its framework version?

## Sources

- [S1] Research report "Software architecture, .NET and Angular" of this project, checklist annex, 2026-11-12, built from the documents of this topic and their sources.

title: Structure of a .NET solution
topic: architecture
summary: Layers or vertical slices, module rules, CQRS written by hand without a mediator library, and when each fits. .NET 10.
tool: dotnet
version: "10"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4]

# Structure of a .NET solution

Contents: 1. Styles · 2. CQRS without a library · 3. Architecture tests · Sources

## 1. Styles inside one application (clean architecture or vertical slices)

| Style | Idea | Fits when | Risk |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Clean architecture | Domain, Application, Infrastructure, Presentation layers; dependencies point inward | Rich domain, rules shared by features, long project | Over-engineering simple features; one feature touches several projects [S1] |
| Vertical slices | Code grouped by request or feature, from interface to data | Features of uneven complexity, small team, mostly CRUD API | Duplication; less consistency; needs a team that refactors [S1] [S2] |
| Mixed | Vertical slices inside a layered solution | The most common case in practice | — [S1] |

Rule of slices: little coupling between slices, much inside a slice. [S2] Remove every layer that only passes a call through. [S2]

## 2. CQRS without a library

- CQRS separates the write model from the read model; its simple form keeps one database; it does not fit a simple domain or a CRUD interface. [S3]
- The pattern needs no mediator: one class per command or query, called directly by the API endpoint. [S3]

```csharp
public sealed class CloseValveHandler(AppDbContext db)
{
    public async Task<Results<NoContent, NotFound>> HandleAsync(string tagName, CancellationToken cancellationToken)
    {
        Valve? valve = await db.Valves.SingleOrDefaultAsync(v => v.TagName == tagName, cancellationToken);
        if (valve is null)
        {
            return TypedResults.NotFound();
        }
        valve.Close();
        await db.SaveChangesAsync(cancellationToken);
        return TypedResults.NoContent();
    }
}

app.MapPost("/valves/{tagName}/close", (string tagName, CloseValveHandler handler, CancellationToken cancellationToken)
    => handler.HandleAsync(tagName, cancellationToken));
```

MediatR 13 and later is no longer free for every company: see `docs/dotnet/paid-packages.md`.

## 3. Architecture tests (.NET)

- Check architecture rules at each build with a test: NetArchTest (MIT) states that "types of this namespace do not depend on that one". [S4]
- Write one test per module rule: no module reads the namespace of the data of another module.

## Sources

- [S1] Milan Jovanović, "Vertical Slice Architecture vs Clean Architecture", 2026-08-13, <https://milanjovanovic.tech/blog/vertical-slice-vs-clean-architecture.md>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Jimmy Bogard, "Vertical Slice Architecture", 2018-04-19, <https://www.jimmybogard.com/vertical-slice-architecture/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] Microsoft, "CQRS pattern", Azure Architecture Center, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/patterns/cqrs>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] Ben Morris, "NetArchTest", README, <https://github.com/BenMorris/NetArchTest>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.

# Reading CMakeLists.txt and Makefile

Contents: 1. CMakeLists.txt · 2. Makefile · 3. What to take before explaining · Sources

## 1. CMakeLists.txt (CMake build)

| You read | What it says about the project | C# landmark |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `add_executable`, `add_library` (`STATIC`, `SHARED`, `MODULE`, `OBJECT`, `INTERFACE`) | The targets: programs and libraries, and their kind [S1] | `OutputType` of a `.csproj` |
| `target_link_libraries(target PRIVATE or PUBLIC or INTERFACE dependency)` | The dependencies; `PUBLIC` and `INTERFACE` pass on to users of the target [S1] | `ProjectReference`, `PackageReference` |
| `target_compile_definitions` | Macros given to the compiler (`-D`): they decide the `#ifdef` branches [S1] | `DefineConstants` |
| `target_compile_features`, `target_include_directories` | The minimum language standard; where headers are found [S1] | `LangVersion` |
| Target `Namespace::name`, `$<…>` expression, `CMAKE_BUILD_TYPE` | Imported target or alias; value computed at generation; configuration (`Debug`, `Release`, `RelWithDebInfo`, `MinSizeRel`) [S1] | Build configuration |

## 2. Makefile (GNU Make)

| You read | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `$@`, `$<`, `$^`, `$?`, `$*` | Target, first prerequisite, all prerequisites, newer prerequisites, pattern stem [S2] |
| `include`, `ifdef`, `ifeq`, `define`, `export` | Another file read; conditions; multi-line variable; variable passed to sub-processes [S2] |
| `$(wildcard …)`, `$(patsubst …)`, `$(shell …)`, `$(foreach …)`, `$(call …)` | Computed file lists and commands [S2] |

The GNU Make manual is under GFDL: described here in own words.

## 3. What to take before explaining (C and C++ build)

- The language standard: `target_compile_features` or the standard property.
- The defined macros: they say which `#ifdef` branch is compiled.
- The dependencies: they explain an unknown name (library, generated code).

## Sources

- [S1] Kitware, "cmake-buildsystem(7)", CMake 4.5.0, <https://cmake.org/cmake/help/latest/manual/cmake-buildsystem.7.html>, read 2026-11-12, BSD 3-Clause, summarized.
- [S2] Free Software Foundation, "Quick Reference", GNU make 4.4.1, <https://www.gnu.org/software/make/manual/html_node/Quick-Reference.html>, read 2026-11-12, GFDL 1.3, summarized in own words.

# Reading pom.xml, build.gradle and Cargo.toml

Contents: 1. pom.xml · 2. build.gradle · 3. Cargo.toml · Sources

## 1. pom.xml (Maven)

| You read | What it says | C# landmark |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `groupId`, `artifactId`, `version`, `packaging` | Identity and output kind (`jar` by default) [S1] | NuGet package identity |
| `parent`; `modules` | Inherited settings; sub-projects built together [S1] | `Directory.Build.props`; solution |
| `properties`, for example `maven.compiler.release` | The Java version targeted [S1] | `TargetFramework` |
| `dependencies` and `scope` (`compile`, `provided`, `runtime`, `test`, `system`); `dependencyManagement` | Libraries and when they serve; central versions [S1] | `PackageReference`; central package management |
| `build` and `plugins`; `profiles` | Build steps, code generation included; variants by environment [S1] | MSBuild targets |

The effective POM, inheritance included, comes from `mvn help:effective-pom`. [S1]

## 2. build.gradle (Gradle)

| You read | What it says | C# landmark |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `plugins { }`; `application { mainClass }` | A Groovy or Kotlin script; plugins add tasks; entry point [S2] | `.csproj`, but it is code |
| `implementation`, `api`, `compileOnly`, `runtimeOnly`, `testImplementation` | `api` is visible to whoever compiles against the library; `implementation` is hidden at compile time [S3] | `PrivateAssets` (close) |
| `libs.guava` | A dependency taken from a version catalog [S2] | Central package management |

## 3. Cargo.toml (Rust)

| You read | What it says | C# landmark |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `[package]`: `name`, `version`, `edition`, `rust-version`, `build` | Identity; language edition (2015 when absent, `cargo new` writes 2024); minimum Rust; build script `build.rs` [S4] | `.csproj` [S5] |
| `[dependencies]`, `[dev-dependencies]`, `[build-dependencies]` | Run, test and build-script libraries [S4] | `PackageReference` |
| `[features]`, `[workspace]`, `[profile.*]`, `[lints]` | Conditional compilation; a set of crates; optimization settings; lint levels [S4] | `DefineConstants`; solution [S5] |

id: reading-code-c-embedded-idioms
title: C embedded idioms
topic: reading-code
summary: Bit-fields, volatile, fixed-point Q format, memory-mapped registers and interrupt handlers in embedded C. C23.
tool: c
version: "C23"

---

# C embedded idioms

Contents: 1. Bit masks and bit-fields · 2. volatile · 3. Fixed-point numbers · 4. Memory-mapped registers · 5. Interrupt handlers · 6. Pitfalls · Sources

## 1. Bit masks and bit-fields (embedded C)

Embedded code packs several small values into one integer. It does this in two ways.

Bit masks use the operators `|`, `&`, `~` and `<<`. They mean the same as in C#. [unverified]

```c
#define FLAG_READY (1u << 0)
#define FLAG_ERROR (1u << 3)

status |= FLAG_READY;          /* set bit 0 */
status &= ~FLAG_ERROR;         /* clear bit 3 */
if (status & FLAG_ERROR) { }   /* test bit 3 */
```

A bit-field is a struct member with a width in bits. [S18]

```c
struct Status {
    unsigned ready : 1;   /* 1 bit */
    unsigned mode  : 3;   /* 3 bits */
    unsigned       : 4;   /* 4 unnamed bits: padding */
};
```

- An unnamed bit-field is padding. An unnamed bit-field of width 0 makes the next member start on a new `int` boundary. [S18]
- You cannot take the address of a bit-field. [S18]
- The compiler decides the order of the bits, the sign of a plain `int` bit-field, and whether a field can cross two storage units. [S18]
- Never say which physical bit a field uses, unless the compiler and the target are known.
- C# equivalent: masks on an integer, or a `[Flags]` enum. C# has no bit-field syntax.

## 2. volatile (embedded C)

`volatile` tells the compiler that a value can change outside the code it sees. The compiler must read the value again at each use. It must not keep a cached copy. [S45]

Code uses `volatile` for three kinds of variables: [S45]

- A hardware register mapped in memory.
- A global variable that an interrupt handler changes.
- A global variable that several tasks share.

```c
volatile uint8_t *status_reg;   /* pointer to volatile data */
uint8_t * volatile cursor;      /* volatile pointer to ordinary data */
```

Read these declarations from right to left. [S45]

- `volatile` does not make an operation atomic. `counter++` on a `volatile` variable is still a read, an add and a write. [unverified]
- C# equivalent: C# has a `volatile` keyword for fields shared between threads. The hardware use has no C# equivalent.

## 3. Fixed-point numbers (Q format)

Code without floating-point hardware stores real numbers in integers. The format Qm.n means: `n` bits hold the fraction. The stored integer is the real value multiplied by 2 to the power `n`. [S46]

- Example with `n` = 8: the stored integer 384 means 384 / 256 = 1.5. [S46]
- Two conventions exist. The TI convention does not count the sign bit in `m`. The ARM convention counts it. [S46]
- "Q15" in 16 bits covers about -1 to just under +1. [S46]
- Addition: add the stored integers. Both must have the same `n`. [S46]
- Multiplication: the raw product has `2n` fraction bits. The code shifts it right by `n` bits. It uses a wider type for the product. It adds a rounding constant before the shift. It saturates the result. [S46]

```c
int16_t q15_mul(int16_t a, int16_t b)
{
    int32_t wide = (int32_t)a * b;   /* product in a wider type */
    wide += 1 << 14;                 /* rounding constant */
    return (int16_t)(wide >> 15);    /* back to Q15; no saturation here */
}
```

- When you see `(a * b) >> 15`, explain it as a fixed-point multiplication, not as a division by 32768.
- C# equivalent: none built in. It is a scaled integer, not `decimal` and not `double`.

## 4. Memory-mapped registers (C and Rust)

A hardware register sits at a fixed address. The code reads and writes it through a pointer to `volatile` data. [S45]

```c
#define TIMER_BASE 0x40001000u                               /* example address */
#define TIMER_CTRL (*(volatile uint32_t *)(TIMER_BASE + 0x00u))

TIMER_CTRL |= 1u << 0;    /* writes to the hardware, not to RAM */
```

- `TIMER_CTRL` looks like a variable. It is a macro that reads and writes hardware.
- Some code uses a struct pointer instead, for example `SCB->VTOR` in CMSIS. [S47]
- The excerpt does not say what a bit does. That is in the hardware manual. Say "not visible in this excerpt".

In embedded Rust, a generated crate gives one object per register: [S43]

- `read()` returns the fields.
- `write(...)` sets the fields you name and puts the others back to their default value.
- `modify(...)` changes only the fields you name.
- `Peripherals::take()` returns an `Option`: each peripheral has exactly one owner.

## 5. Interrupt handlers (CMSIS and Rust)

An interrupt handler is a function that the hardware calls. No code calls it. [S47]

```c
volatile uint32_t tick_count;        /* shared with the main code */

void SysTick_Handler(void)           /* the name is fixed by the vector table */
{
    tick_count++;
}
```

- In CMSIS, the startup file holds the vector table. Core handlers have fixed names such as `SysTick_Handler`. Device handlers end with `_IRQHandler`. [S47]
- The default handlers are weak. A function with the same name replaces the default. [S47]
- `NVIC_EnableIRQ` turns a device interrupt on. `NVIC_SetPriority` sets its priority: a lower value means a higher priority. [S47]
- A variable shared between a handler and the main code is declared `volatile`. [S45]
- In embedded Rust, a handler has the attribute `#[interrupt]` and the name of the interrupt. Other code cannot call it. [S44]
- A handler must clear the cause of the interrupt. If it does not, the handler can run again without end. [S44]
- C# equivalent: an event handler that the runtime calls. There is no closer match.

## 6. Pitfalls (reading embedded C)

- A handler with no caller is not dead code. [S47]
- Never merge two reads of a `volatile` object into one in your explanation. Each read is real. [S45]
- The layout of a bit-field depends on the compiler. [S18]
- The value of a fixed-point integer depends on its Q format. If the format is not shown, ask for it. [S46]
- Handler names and startup files in this document follow CMSIS for Arm Cortex-M. Other targets use other names. [unverified]

## Sources

- [S18] Microsoft, "C Bit Fields", updated 2023-07-26, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/c-language/c-bit-fields>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S43] Rust Embedded, "Memory Mapped Registers", The Embedded Rust Book, <https://docs.rust-embedded.org/book/start/registers.html>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.
- [S44] Rust Embedded, "Interrupts", The Embedded Rust Book, <https://docs.rust-embedded.org/book/start/interrupts.html>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.
- [S45] N. Jones, "How to Use C's volatile Keyword", Barr Group, 2001, <https://barrgroup.com/blog/how-use-cs-volatile-keyword>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.
- [S46] Wikipedia, "Q (number format)", <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Q_(number_format)>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA 4.0, summarized.
- [S47] Arm, "Interrupts and Exceptions (NVIC)", CMSIS-Core, CMSIS 6, <https://arm-software.github.io/CMSIS_6/latest/Core/group__NVIC__gr.html>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.

# C for C# readers

Contents: 0. Summary · 1. False friends · 2. Pointers · 3. Arrays and strings · 4. Manual memory · 5. Headers · 6. Macros · 7. Undefined behavior · 8. Pitfalls · Old patterns · Sources

## 0. Summary (C constructs by kind)

Kind: "same meaning" reads as in C#; "false friend" looks like C# but means something else; "no equivalent" has no C# counterpart.

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `int tab[10]; f(tab);` | `f` gets a pointer to the first element; the length is lost [S16] | An array that knows its length | false friend |
| `#include "x.h"` | The text of `x.h` is pasted before compilation [S17] | `using` | false friend |
| `MAX(a, b)` in capitals | Maybe a macro: text replacement, an argument can run twice [S21] | A method call | false friend |
| `if (x = 5)` | Compiles: assigns 5, then tests the value [S12] | A compile error | false friend |
| `int *p = &x;` | `p` holds the address of `x` [S16] | `ref int p = ref x;` | same meaning |
| `malloc` and `free` | Manual memory: the code frees what it allocates | None: the garbage collector | no equivalent |
| `n + 1` on the largest `int` | Undefined behavior [S19] | The value wraps around | false friend |
| `struct` with fields | Plain data, no methods | `struct` | same meaning |

## 1. False friends (C syntax that looks like C#)

These lines look like C#. They mean something else. Check them first.

| You read | In C# you expect | In C it means |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `int tab[10]; f(tab);` | The array knows its length. | `f` gets a pointer to the first element. The length is lost. [S16] |
| `int *p;` then `*p = 1;` | A compile error. | `p` holds a random address. The write goes anywhere. [S16] |
| `#include "x.h"` | Something like `using`. | The text of `x.h` is pasted here before compilation. [S17] |
| `MAX(a, b)` | A method call. | Maybe a macro: text replacement. An argument can run twice. [S21] |
| `if (x = 5)` | A compile error. | It compiles. It assigns 5 to `x`, then tests the value. [S12] |
| `n + 1` when `n` is the largest `int` | The value wraps around. | Undefined behavior. [S19] |
| `char c` | A UTF-16 character. | A one-byte integer. [unverified] |
| `static int n;` at file level | A class member. | A variable visible in this file only. [unverified] |

## 2. Pointers (C pointers)

A pointer stores the address of an object. C does not check a pointer: no null check, no bounds check, no lifetime check. [S16]

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `int *p = &x;` | `p` holds the address of `x`. | `ref int p = ref x;` |
| `*p = 3;` | Write 3 into the object that `p` points to. | Assign through a `ref` local. |
| `s->len` | Member `len` of the object that `s` points to. | `s.len` |
| `p = nullptr;` (older code: `NULL`) | `p` points to nothing. [S22] | `null` |
| `p + 1`, `p++` | Address of the next element, not of the next byte. [S16] | `span.Slice(1)` |
| `void *p` | An address with no type. Cast it before use. [S16] | `IntPtr` |
| `int (*cb)(int)` | Pointer to a function, used as a callback. [S16] | `Func<int, int>` |
| `const int *p` | You cannot write through `p`. [S16] | `ref readonly int` |
| `int *const p` | You cannot make `p` point elsewhere. [S16] | No equivalent. |

Read a declaration from right to left. `const int *p` reads: `p` is a pointer to an `int` that is `const`. [S45]

A pointer that was never assigned holds a random address. [S16]

## 3. Arrays and strings (C arrays)

An array passed to a function becomes a pointer to its first element. Inside the function, `sizeof` gives the size of the pointer, not of the array. The code must pass the length as a second argument. [S16]

```c
void clear(int *dst, int count)       /* the length comes separately */
{
    for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) {
        dst[i] = 0;                   /* same as *(dst + i) = 0 */
    }
}
```

- C does not check the index. A read or a write outside the array is undefined behavior. [S19]
- A string is an array of `char` that ends with a zero byte. It has no length field. [unverified]
- C# equivalent: `int[]` and `string` know their length. `Span<T>` is the closest type to "pointer plus length".

## 4. Manual memory (C malloc and free)

C has no garbage collector. The code asks for a block of memory and must give it back exactly once.

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `p = malloc(n);` | Ask for `n` bytes. The bytes are not set to zero. [S19] | `new byte[n]`, which is zeroed |
| `free(p);` | Give the block back. `p` still holds the old address. [unverified] | Nothing: the garbage collector does it |

- Memory that is never freed is a leak. Freeing the same block twice crashes the program. [S16]
- When you explain C code, say who owns each block and where it is freed. If the excerpt does not show the `free`, say so.

## 5. Headers (C #include)

Each source file is compiled alone. A header (`.h`) holds the declarations that several source files share. `#include` pastes the text of the header into the file before compilation. [S17]

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `int area(int w, int h);` in a `.h` file | A declaration: the function exists somewhere. [S17] | No equivalent: C# needs no declaration |
| The same function with a body, in one `.c` file | The definition. There must be exactly one in the program. [S17] | The method itself |
| `#ifndef AREA_H`, `#define AREA_H`, `#endif` | Include guard: stops a second paste of the same header. [S17] | Not needed |
| `#pragma once` | Same purpose as an include guard. [S17] | Not needed |

## 6. Macros (C preprocessor)

The preprocessor rewrites the text before the compiler reads it. A macro is a text replacement, not a function. [S20]

```c
#define SQUARE(x) x * x
int a = SQUARE(1 + 2);        /* becomes 1 + 2 * 1 + 2, which is 5, not 9 */

#define MAX(a, b) ((a) > (b) ? (a) : (b))
int m = MAX(i++, j);          /* i++ can run twice */
```

- Known macro pitfalls: operator precedence, side effects that run twice, a swallowed semicolon, bad nesting. [S21]
- CERT C rule PRE31-C is about side effects in macro arguments. [S12]
- `#ifdef NAME` keeps or removes code. The build decides: the option `-D NAME` defines the macro. [S20]
- To see the code after the preprocessor: `gcc -E -P file.c`. To list the defined macros: add `-dM`. [S20]
- C# equivalent: `#if` works the same way. `#define` in C# cannot hold a value or take arguments.

## 7. Undefined behavior (C)

For some operations, the C standard sets no result at all. The compiler assumes these cases never happen and optimizes on that basis. The program can do anything. [S19]

| You read | In C | In C# |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A local variable read before any write | A random value | Compile error |
| A signed `int` that overflows | Undefined. An unsigned type wraps. | Wraps, or throws in a `checked` context |
| A shift by more bits than the type has | Undefined | The shift count is masked |
| `*p` when `p` is null or invalid | Undefined. It may not crash. | `NullReferenceException` |
| An index outside the array | Undefined | `IndexOutOfRangeException` |
| Data read through a pointer of another type | Undefined | Not possible in safe code |

All C rows: [S19].

## 8. Pitfalls (reading C)

- Never describe the "result" of undefined behavior. Say that it is undefined. [S19]
- A name in capitals followed by `(` is probably a macro. Find its `#define` before you explain the line. [unverified]
- Code inside `#ifdef` may not be compiled at all. Say which macro decides. [S20]
- A function that returns a pointer: say who must free the memory. If the excerpt does not show it, say "not visible in this excerpt".

## Old patterns

- Function definitions with the parameter types listed after the parenthesis (before C23). Removed in C23. [S22]
- `NULL` for a null pointer (before C23). C23 adds `nullptr`. [S22]
- `true`, `false`, `static_assert` and `thread_local` as macros (before C23). They are keywords in C23. [S22]

## Sources

- [S12] Carnegie Mellon University, SEI, "SEI CERT C Coding Standard", 2016 edition, <https://resources.sei.cmu.edu/downloads/secure-coding/assets/sei-cert-c-coding-standard-2016-v01.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, all rights reserved, rule identifiers cited.
- [S16] Microsoft, "Raw pointers (C++)", updated 2025-02-21, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/cpp/raw-pointers>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S17] Microsoft, "Header files (C++)", updated 2021-08-03, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/cpp/header-files-cpp>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S19] C. Lattner, "What Every C Programmer Should Know About Undefined Behavior #1/3", 2011, <https://blog.llvm.org/2011/05/what-every-c-programmer-should-know.html>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.
- [S20] GCC, "Options Controlling the Preprocessor", <https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/gcc/Preprocessor-Options.html>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.
- [S21] GCC, "Macro Pitfalls", <https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/cpp/Macro-Pitfalls.html>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.
- [S22] cppreference.com, "C23", <https://en.cppreference.com/w/c/23>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA 3.0 and GFDL, summarized.
- [S45] N. Jones, "How to Use C's volatile Keyword", Barr Group, 2001, <https://barrgroup.com/blog/how-use-cs-volatile-keyword>, read 2026-11-12, license not stated, summarized.

tool: cpp
version: "C++23"

# C++ for C# readers

Contents: 1. Lifetime and ownership · 2. Templates and overloads · 3. Modern syntax · 4. Pitfalls · Sources

Kind: "same meaning" reads as in C#; "false friend" looks like C# but means something else; "no equivalent" has no C# counterpart. Read the false friends first. C++23 is the published standard (ISO/IEC 14882:2024). [S8]

## 1. Lifetime and ownership (C++)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A local object whose destructor `~T()` frees a resource | RAII: the resource is released when the scope ends, even on an exception [S1] [S2] | `using` and `Dispose`, but automatic | no equivalent |
| `std::unique_ptr<T>` | One owner only; it moves, it never copies [S2] | None; the garbage collector has no such role | no equivalent |
| `std::shared_ptr<T>` | Reference counting; the object dies with its last owner, at once [S2] | A plain C# reference, but destruction is immediate | false friend |
| `std::weak_ptr<T>` | Observes without owning; breaks cycles [S2] | `WeakReference<T>` | same meaning |
| `p.get()` | A raw pointer lent to an old API; ownership does not change [S2] | — | no equivalent |
| `T&&` in a signature | Rvalue reference: marks a move constructor or move assignment [S3] | None | no equivalent |
| `std::move(x)` | A cast: it moves nothing; the move happens in the constructor called; `x` stays valid but emptied [S3] | None | false friend |

## 2. Templates and overloads (C++)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `template<typename T>` | Code checked at each instantiation, for each type [S4] | `class Foo<T>`: substitution at run time, operations limited by constraints [S4] | false friend |
| `template<int N>`; `template<> struct X<int>` | A value parameter; a specialization: another implementation for one type [S4] | None [S4] | no equivalent |
| Two functions with the same name | Overload chosen by rank: exact match, promotion, standard conversion, user conversion, ellipsis; ambiguity is an error [S5] | C# overloads, other rules | false friend |
| A same-named function in a derived class | It hides every overload of the base class, unless it overrides a virtual function [S5] | — | false friend |

The function a C++ call reaches depends on declarations: name it only when they are visible in the excerpt.

## 3. Modern syntax (C++)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `auto x = …;`; `for (auto& e : v)` | Deduced type; range loop [S1] | `var`; `foreach` | same meaning |
| `[…](int x) { … }` | Lambda; the brackets list what is captured [S1] [S6] | C# lambda, implicit capture | same meaning |
| `constexpr` | Constant computed at compile time [S1] | `const` | same meaning |
| `std::string_view`, `std::span` | A view without copy or ownership [S1] [S6] | `ReadOnlySpan<char>`, `Span<T>` | same meaning |
| `std::variant`, `std::optional`, `std::expected` | Safe union; value or nothing; value or error (C++23) [S1] [S6] | No native union; `T?` | no equivalent |
| `import` (C++20) | Module: in time it replaces headers [S7] | `using` | same meaning |

To date an excerpt by its syntax, see `docs/reading-code/cpp-version-markers.md`.

## 4. Pitfalls (reading C++)

- A missing destructor call does not exist: the end of scope runs it. Say where each resource is released.
- After `std::move(x)`, do not describe `x` as unchanged nor as destroyed: it is valid and emptied.
- Overloads and templates: when the declarations are not in the excerpt, write "depends on declarations not visible in this excerpt".
- The C++ Core Guidelines cannot be copied into a public base: describe in own words.

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft, "Welcome back to C++, Modern C++", 2026, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/cpp/welcome-back-to-cpp-modern-cpp>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] Microsoft, "Smart pointers (Modern C++)", 2026, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/cpp/smart-pointers-modern-cpp>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S3] Microsoft, "Move Constructors and Move Assignment Operators (C++)", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/cpp/move-constructors-and-move-assignment-operators-cpp>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S4] Microsoft, "Differences Between C++ Templates and C# Generics", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/programming-guide/generics/differences-between-cpp-templates-and-csharp-generics>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S5] Microsoft, "Function overloading", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/cpp/function-overloading>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S6] Anthony Calandra, "modern-cpp-features", <https://github.com/AnthonyCalandra/modern-cpp-features>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S7] Microsoft, "Header files (C++)", <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/cpp/cpp/header-files-cpp>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S8] Standard C++ Foundation, "The Standard", <https://isocpp.org/std/the-standard>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# C++ version markers

Contents: 1. Markers · 2. How to use · Sources

## 1. Markers by standard (C++11 to C++23)

| Standard | Markers |
| --- | --- |
| C++11 | `auto`, lambdas, `nullptr`, `enum class`, range `for`, `constexpr`, `override` and `final`, `= default` and `= delete`, `noexcept`, move and `&&`, variadic templates, raw strings |
| C++14 | Generic lambdas, binary literals, return type deduction, `[[deprecated]]` |
| C++17 | Structured bindings, `if constexpr`, `if (init; cond)`, nested namespaces `A::B`, `inline` variables, `[[nodiscard]]`, class template argument deduction |
| C++20 | Concepts, coroutines, `<=>` comparison, designated initializers, `consteval`, `constinit`, `using enum`, `std::span`, text formatting, `std::jthread` |
| C++23 | Deducing `this`, `if consteval`, multi-index `operator[]`, `std::expected`, `std::to_underlying`, `std::unreachable`, the `stacktrace` library |

Source: [S1]. C++23 is the published standard (ISO/IEC 14882:2024); C++26 is in progress. [S2]

## 2. How to use the markers (dating an excerpt)

- The newest marker found gives the oldest standard the excerpt needs.
- The build file gives the real standard: `target_compile_features` or `CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD` in `CMakeLists.txt` (`docs/reading-code/build-cmake-make.md`).
- No marker found: say that the excerpt could be C++98 or later.

## Sources

- [S1] Anthony Calandra, "modern-cpp-features", <https://github.com/AnthonyCalandra/modern-cpp-features>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S2] Standard C++ Foundation, "The Standard", <https://isocpp.org/std/the-standard>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: reading-code-java-for-csharp-readers
title: Java for C# readers
topic: reading-code
summary: Erased generics, checked exceptions, records, sealed types, streams and annotations in Java, with C# equivalents. Java 25.
tool: java
version: "25"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6, S7]
license: MIT (own words and own examples; facts summarized from the sources)
variant: chat

# Java for C# readers

Contents: 1. Close to C#, different underneath · 2. Recent syntax · 3. Pitfalls · Sources

Kind: "same meaning" reads as in C#; "false friend" looks like C# but means something else; "no equivalent" has no C# counterpart. Java shares the C syntax family, objects, strong typing, a garbage collector, `try`/`catch`/`finally` and `var` with C#. [S1] The language changes read go up to Java 25. [S3]

## 1. Close to C#, different underneath (Java)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `List<String>` | Generic erased at run time: no `new T()`, no `instanceof List<String>`, no primitive type argument (`List<Integer>`, never `List<int>`), no overloads that differ only by type argument [S2] | C# generics keep their type at run time; TypeScript types are erased too | false friend |
| `void f() throws IOException` | Checked exception: the caller must catch it or declare it [S1] | None: any C# method may throw any exception [S1] | no equivalent |
| `a == b` on two `String` | Compares references, not content [S7] | `==` on `string` compares content in C# | false friend |
| `final` | Constant or variable that cannot be reassigned [S1] | `const`, `readonly` | same meaning |
| `getName()`, `setName(…)` | A property by naming convention [S1] | Property `Name { get; set; }` [S1] | same meaning |
| `list.stream().filter(…).map(…).toList()` | Stream: nothing runs before the terminal operation; a stream is traversed once only [S4] | LINQ `Where`, `Select`, `ToList` [S1] | same meaning |
| `Optional<T>` and `.orElse(x)` | Value or absence [S1] | `??` [S1] | same meaning |
| `@Override`, `@Deprecated` | Annotations; `@Override` makes the compiler check the override [S1] [S5] | `override`, `[Obsolete]` | same meaning |
| `@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)` | The annotation stays readable at run time; by default the JVM does not see it [S5] | — | no equivalent |

## 2. Recent syntax (Java 15 to 25)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `record Point(int x, int y) {}` | Record, final since Java 16 [S3] | `record` [S1] | same meaning |
| `sealed interface S permits A, B` | Closed hierarchy, since Java 17 [S3] | No direct equivalent | no equivalent |
| `switch (o) { case Circle c -> … }` | Pattern matching in `switch`, since Java 21 [S3] | `switch` expression with patterns | same meaning |
| `"""…"""` | Text block, since Java 15 [S3] | Raw string `"""` | same meaning |
| `_` as a name | Unnamed variable, since Java 22 [S3] | Discard `_` | same meaning |
| `import module java.base;` | Import of a whole module, since Java 25 [S3] | — | no equivalent |

## 3. Pitfalls (reading Java)

- An annotation can make a framework create objects, methods or proxies that are not in the file: see `docs/reading-code/java-framework-idioms.md`.
- The Java version is in the build file: `maven.compiler.release` in `pom.xml` (`docs/reading-code/build-maven-gradle-cargo.md`).
- The Java SE documentation cannot be copied into a public base: describe in own words. [S6]

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft, "Tips for Java developers", 2026, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/csharp/tour-of-csharp/tips-for-java-developers>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] Oracle, "Restrictions on Generics", The Java Tutorials, <https://docs.oracle.com/javase/tutorial/java/generics/restrictions.html>, read 2026-11-12, all rights reserved, summarized in own words.
- [S3] Oracle, "Java Language Changes", Java SE 25, <https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/25/language/java-language-changes-release.html>, read 2026-11-12, all rights reserved, summarized in own words.
- [S4] Oracle, "Package java.util.stream", Java SE 25 API, <https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/25/docs/api/java.base/java/util/stream/package-summary.html>, read 2026-11-12, all rights reserved, summarized in own words.
- [S5] Oracle, "Predefined Annotation Types", The Java Tutorials, <https://docs.oracle.com/javase/tutorial/java/annotations/predefined.html>, read 2026-11-12, all rights reserved, summarized in own words.
- [S6] Oracle, "JSR-000384 Java SE 11 Final Release", specification license, <https://www.oracle.com/a/tech/docs/java-se-11-final-spec-license.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S7] Du, Schantong, Siegmund, "Knowledge Transfer and False Friends: Insights on Transitioning from C to Java", Chemnitz University of Technology, <https://tu-chemnitz.de/informatik/ST/publications/papers/Transfer.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: reading-code-java-framework-idioms
title: Java framework idioms
topic: reading-code
summary: What Spring and Lombok annotations do without showing it in the code, and the C# landmark of each.
tool: java
version: "25"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2]
license: MIT (own words; facts summarized from the sources)
variant: chat

# Java framework idioms

Contents: 1. Spring · 2. Lombok · 3. How to explain · Sources

## 1. Spring annotations (hidden object creation)

| You read | What happens without showing | C# landmark |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `@Component`, `@Service`, `@Repository`, `@Controller` | Spring scans the classes, registers those with these annotations, creates the object and passes its dependencies: no `new` in the code [S1] | Registration in the ASP.NET Core dependency container |
| `@Configuration` and `@Bean` | A factory method that declares a managed object [S1] | Registration by factory |
| `@Autowired`, `@Qualifier`, `@Scope` | Injection point; choice between candidates; scope, singleton by default [S1] | Constructor injection, service lifetime |

## 2. Lombok annotations (generated members)

| You read | What happens without showing | C# landmark |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `@Getter`, `@Setter` | Accessors generated for the fields [S2] | Auto-properties |
| `@Data` | Accessors, `equals`, `hashCode` and `toString` generated [S2] | `record` |
| `@Builder` | A builder class generated [S2] | Source generator |
| `@RequiredArgsConstructor` | A constructor for the final fields generated [S2] | Primary constructor |

The code calls methods that are not written in the file: say which annotation creates them. Other frameworks (JPA, JUnit, Jakarta EE) were not checked for this base.

## 3. How to explain a framework class

- Name the annotations first, then what each one creates or injects.
- An object with no `new` in the excerpt is created by the framework: never call it unused.
- When the framework version matters, read it in the build file.

## Sources

- [S1] Spring, "Classpath Scanning and Managed Components", Spring Framework 7.0.10, <https://docs.spring.io/spring-framework/reference/core/beans/classpath-scanning.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Project Lombok, "Lombok features", <https://projectlombok.org/features/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: reading-code-rust-for-csharp-readers
title: Rust for C# readers
topic: reading-code
summary: Ownership, borrowing, lifetimes, traits, Option, Result and the question mark in Rust, with C# equivalents. Edition 2024.
tool: rust

# Rust for C# readers

Contents: 1. Ownership · 2. Types and traits · 3. Errors · 4. Collections and modules · Sources

Kind: "same meaning" reads as in C#; "false friend" looks like C# but means something else; "no equivalent" has no C# counterpart. Ownership replaces the garbage collector; `Result` replaces exceptions. The 2024 edition came with Rust 1.85.0. [S5]

## 1. Ownership and lifetimes (Rust)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `let s2 = s1;` | Move: `s1` can no longer be used, a compile error otherwise [S2] | Both variables stay valid in C# | false friend |
| `f(s)` | Passing a value moves it too, except `Copy` types (integers, `bool`, floats, `char`) [S2] | — | false friend |
| `s1.clone()` | Explicit deep copy [S2] | `Clone()` | same meaning |
| End of block `}` | The value is dropped when its owner leaves scope [S2] | `IDisposable` and `using`, but automatic [S1] | no equivalent |
| `fn f<'a>(x: &'a str) -> &'a str` | Lifetime: links reference durations for the compiler; changes nothing at run time [S3] | None | no equivalent |
| `&'static str` | A reference valid for the whole program, a literal for example [S3] | — | no equivalent |

## 2. Types and traits (Rust)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `struct` and `impl` | Data, then methods in a separate block; no inheritance [S1] | `class` | same meaning |
| `trait` and `impl Trait for Type` | Interface, with possible default methods [S1] | `interface` [S1] | same meaning |
| `#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]` | Code generated by a macro; value equality comes from `PartialEq` [S1] | Members synthesized for a `record` | no equivalent |
| `enum E { A(i32), B }` and `match` | Each variant can carry data [S1] | No direct equivalent; TypeScript discriminated union | false friend |
| `Option<T>`, `Some(v)`, `None` | No `null`; `map` and `and_then` for `?.`, `unwrap_or` for `??`, `unwrap` for `!` [S1] | `T?` | same meaning |

## 3. Errors (Rust)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `Result<T, E>`, `Ok(v)`, `Err(e)` | No exceptions: the error is in the signature [S1] [S4] | `try`/`catch` | no equivalent |
| `expr?` | On `Err`, returns at once from the function, after converting the error with `From` [S4] | Rethrow; nothing to do with `?.` | false friend |
| `.unwrap()`, `.expect("…")`, `panic!` | Stop on error; `panic!` signals a bug, not an expected error [S4] [S1] | Unhandled exception | same meaning |

## 4. Collections and modules (Rust)

| You read | Meaning | C# equivalent | Kind |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `.iter().filter(…).map(…).collect()` | Lazy iterators; iterating a `Vec` by value consumes it [S1] | LINQ `Where`, `Select`, `ToList` [S1] | same meaning |
| `mod`, `pub`, `pub(crate)` | Module; private by default; `pub(crate)` is `internal` [S1] | `namespace`, `internal` | same meaning |
| `#[cfg(test)] mod tests`, `#[test]` | Unit tests in the same file [S1] | A separate test project | no equivalent |

Macros, `unsafe` and `async` are in `docs/reading-code/rust-macros-unsafe-async.md`. The full borrowing rules were not read in an open page for this base.

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft and contributors, "Rust for C#/.NET Developers", <https://microsoft.github.io/rust-for-dotnet-devs/latest/print.html>, read 2026-11-12, MIT-like license, adapted.
- [S2] Klabnik, Nichols, Krycho, "What Is Ownership?", The Rust Programming Language, <https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch04-01-what-is-ownership.html>, read 2026-11-12, MIT or Apache 2.0, summarized.
- [S3] Same authors, "Validating References with Lifetimes", <https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch10-03-lifetime-syntax.html>, read 2026-11-12, MIT or Apache 2.0, summarized.
- [S4] Same authors, "Recoverable Errors with Result", <https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch09-02-recoverable-errors-with-result.html>, read 2026-11-12, MIT or Apache 2.0, summarized.
- [S5] Rust project, "Rust 2024", The Rust Edition Guide, <https://doc.rust-lang.org/edition-guide/rust-2024/index.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: reading-code-rust-macros-unsafe-async
title: Rust macros, unsafe and async
topic: reading-code
summary: Macros, unsafe blocks, foreign functions, async futures and memory-mapped registers in embedded Rust, with C# landmarks.
tool: rust
version: "2024"
checked: 2026-11-13

license: MIT (own words; facts summarized from the sources)
