# Component model interface

Contents: 1. Variable roles · 2. Life cycle · 3. C# template · 4. Link with symbol properties · Sources

## 1. Variable roles of a model (FMI 3.0 causality)

| Role | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `parameter` | Given before the run; constant, unless tunable |
| `calculatedParameter` | Computed at initialisation or when a tunable parameter changes |
| `input` | Given by the importer, before the step |
| `output` | Used by the importer, after the step |
| `local` | Internal (continuous state, debug value); never linked to another model |
| `independent` | The independent variable, usually time; one per model |

Variability says when a value can change: `constant`, `fixed`, `tunable`, `discrete`, `continuous`. [S1] ECSS SMP says the same with state, input and output flags on each published field. [S2]

## 2. Life cycle, seen by three references

| Step | FMI 3.0 [S1] | ECSS SMP [S2] | Trick [S3] [S4] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Create and describe | Instantiation; `modelDescription.xml` | `Publish`, `Configure` | Default data, input file |
| Connect | The importer links outputs to inputs | `Connect`, link registry | Simulation definition file |
| Initialise | Initialisation mode | Initialising state | Initialisation jobs |
| Step | `fmi3DoStep`, or derivatives to an outer solver | Entry points called by the scheduler | Derivative, integration, scheduled jobs |
| Save, restore | Get, set, serialize the FMU state | Storing and restoring states | Jobs around the checkpoint |
| Reset, end | `fmi3Reset`, free the instance | Exiting, aborting | Shutdown jobs |

## 3. C# template of a component model

The smallest interface that covers the three references: parameters set before the run, inputs written before the step, a step that reads neither the clock nor the network, outputs read after the step, a full state that can be read and written, faults and forcings inside the state.

```csharp
public interface IModel
{
    void Initialize();
    void Step(long stepTicks);
    ModelState Save();
    void Restore(ModelState state);
}

public sealed record PumpParameters(double RatedFlowM3PerH, double StartTimeS);

public sealed record PumpState(double FlowRateM3PerH, double StartElapsedS, bool HasTripFault);

public sealed class PumpModel(PumpParameters parameters) : IModel
{
    private PumpState state = new(0, 0, false);

    public bool IsRunCommanded { get; set; }
    public double FlowRateM3PerH => state.FlowRateM3PerH;

    public void Initialize() => state = new PumpState(0, 0, false);

    public void Step(long stepTicks)
    {
        double dt = stepTicks / (double)TimeSpan.TicksPerSecond;
        bool running = IsRunCommanded && !state.HasTripFault;
        double elapsed = running ? state.StartElapsedS + dt : 0;
        double ratio = Math.Min(1.0, elapsed / parameters.StartTimeS);
        state = state with { StartElapsedS = elapsed, FlowRateM3PerH = parameters.RatedFlowM3PerH * ratio };
    }

    public ModelState Save() => ModelState.From(state);

    public void Restore(ModelState saved) => state = saved.To<PumpState>();
}
```

`ModelState` stands for the serialization of the project; the instructor sets `HasTripFault` through a restore or a fault command.

## 4. Link with the symbol properties (synoptics)

What the operator sets is an input; what he reads is an output; what describes the equipment is a parameter; what the instructor breaks is a fault. Name them with `docs/synoptics/symbol-property-naming.md`; the catalogs give the properties of each equipment.

## Sources

- [S1] Modelica Association, "Functional Mock-up Interface Specification", version 3.0, 2022-05-10, <https://fmi-standard.org/docs/3.0>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] ECSS, "Simulation modelling platform", ECSS-E-ST-40-07C Rev.1, 2025-08-05, <https://ecss.nl/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/ECSS-E-ST-40-07C-Rev.1(5August2025).pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] NASA, Trick documentation, "Executive Scheduler", <https://nasa.github.io/trick/documentation/simulation_capabilities/Executive-Scheduler>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] Penn, Lin, "The Trick Simulation Toolkit", NASA NTRS 20150021786, <https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20150021786/downloads/20150021786.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

topic: simulation
summary: HLA (IEEE 1516-2025), DIS (IEEE 1278.1-2012) and DDS 1.4, their terms, services and when to use each.
tool: none
version: "none"

# HLA, DIS and DDS

Contents: 1. Compared · 2. What each brings · 3. When to use · Sources

## 1. HLA, DIS and DDS compared

| Criterion | HLA (IEEE 1516-2025) | DIS (IEEE 1278.1-2012) | DDS 1.4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What it links | Simulators and tools (federates) in a federation, through a RTI | Platform simulators, in real time | The applications of one system |
| Data model | Set by the federation (FOM, XML), modular | Fixed: 72 message types (PDUs) in 13 families | Typed topics |
| Time | Time management services: regulating or constrained federate, lookahead, time advance request and grant | Real time; dead reckoning between two messages | Not in the pages read |
| Freeze, save | Synchronization points, federation save and restore | Start, resume, stop, freeze messages | Not in the pages read |
| Transport | Depends on the RTI (TCP or multicast) | UDP broadcast, multicast | Peer-to-peer publish-subscribe, discovery, quality of service |
| Bodies | IEEE, SISO | IEEE, SISO | OMG |

Sources: [S1] [S2] [S3] [S4] [S5] [S6]. HLA and DIS texts are paid and were not read.

## 2. What each one brings (distributed simulation)

- HLA: seven service groups (federation, declaration, object, ownership, time, data distribution, support) and ten rules; recommended by NATO (STANAG 4603); HLA 4 approved by IEEE in early 2025, with security, scaling and cloud readiness announced. [S1] [S2]
- DIS: from SIMNET; a fixed object model where each simulator broadcasts the state of its entities (Entity State, Fire, Detonation); the RPR FOM maps DIS messages to HLA classes; dead reckoning predicts a position from the last known state and cuts traffic. [S2] [S3] [S4]
- DDS: data-centric middleware; each application reads and writes a local data space, synchronized peer to peer; quality of service sets reliability, liveliness, filtering and deadlines. [S5] [S6]
- Known limit of HLA: no interface for end-to-end deadlines nor system priorities; its callback wait is a source of non-determinism; research adds fixed-priority scheduling, memory locking and CPU affinity for hard real time. [S7]

## 3. When to use each (distributed simulation)

| Need | Choice |
| --- | --- |
| Link the simulator to those of other teams or nations, with a common logical time | HLA |
| Exchange positions and events with existing simulators in real time, without logical time | DIS |
| Distribute data between applications of one system, with delivery guarantees | DDS |
| Let the models of one simulator talk, in one process | None: a function call and a table of variables |

## Sources

- [S1] SISO, "HLA 4 Approved by IEEE", 2025-03-11, <https://www.sisostandards.org/news/695782/HLA-4-Approved-by-IEEE.htm>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Wikipedia, "High Level Architecture", <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Level_Architecture>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA 4.0, summarized.
- [S3] Wikipedia, "Distributed Interactive Simulation", <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distributed_Interactive_Simulation>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA 4.0, summarized.
- [S4] Wikipedia, "Dead reckoning", <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_reckoning>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA 4.0, summarized.
- [S5] DDS Foundation (OMG), "What is DDS?", <https://www.dds-foundation.org/what-is-dds-3/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S6] OpenDDS Foundation, "Introduction to DDS", <https://opendds.readthedocs.io/en/master/devguide/introduction_to_dds.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S7] Adelantado, Siron, Chaudron, "Towards an HLA Run-time Infrastructure with Hard Real-time Capabilities", paper 10E-SIW-011, <https://cdimage.debian.org/mirror/gnu.org/savannah/certi/papers/10E-SIW-011.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# Testing a simulation model

Contents: 1. Tests from the references · 2. Tests of a training simulator · 3. Verification and validation · Sources

## 1. Tests from the references (model testing)

| Test | What it checks |
| --- | --- |
| Unit test | One component alone, for example a consistent initialisation [S4] |
| Numerical reference | The result is compared to a kept reference, with absolute and relative tolerances [S4] |
| Order of convergence | The error falls as expected when the step gets finer [S4] |
| Step convergence | Two runs, the second finer: no significant difference [S1] |
| Comparison with another model | The results follow an analytic model or a valid simulation model [S3] |
| Degenerate and extreme conditions | The model stays logical with an extreme value or an unlikely combination [S3] |
| Sensitivity | The outputs answer a change of input as the real system would [S3] |
| Non-regression | Representative runs are played again after each change [S5] |

## 2. Tests of a training simulator (model testing)

Three tests follow from the time rules and the training functions:

- Determinism: the same inputs twice give the same outputs, step by step.
- Save and restore: save, restore, go on; the outputs equal those of a run without a cut.
- Freeze: freeze, wait, run again; the outputs do not change.

```csharp
[Fact]
public void Restore_then_step_gives_the_same_outputs_as_an_unbroken_run()
{
    PumpModel unbroken = NewRunningPump();
    PumpModel restored = NewRunningPump();
    Step(unbroken, 10);
    Step(restored, 5);
    ModelState saved = restored.Save();
    restored.Restore(saved);
    Step(restored, 5);
    Assert.Equal(unbroken.FlowRateM3PerH, restored.FlowRateM3PerH);
}
```

## 3. Verification and validation (V&V of a model)

- Verification: the program implements the model correctly; validation: the model represents the real system accurately enough for its use. [S2]
- Set the required accuracy of the outputs before development; compare with the real system on several sets of conditions; write it down; review it periodically. [S2]
- Acceptance of a training simulator: tests against plant reference data, with acceptance criteria, initial conditions and expected operator actions; integrated, malfunction and beyond-design groups; pre-factory, factory and site steps. [S5]

## Sources

- [S1] MathWorks, "Check and Improve Simulation Accuracy", Simulink documentation, <https://au.mathworks.com/help/simulink/ug/improving-simulation-accuracy.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Sargent, "An Introductory Tutorial on Verification and Validation of Simulation Models", Winter Simulation Conference 2015, <https://www.informs-sim.org/wsc15papers/182.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] Sargent, "Verification and Validation of Simulation Models", Winter Simulation Conference 2005, <https://www.informs-sim.org/wsc05papers/014.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] CADET, "CADET-Core testing", developer guide, <https://cadet.github.io/master/developer_guide/testing.html>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S5] IAEA, "Guidelines for upgrade and modernization of nuclear power plant training simulators", IAEA-TECDOC-1500, 2006, <https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/Publications/PDF/te_1500_web.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

---
id: simulation-principles
title: Simulation principles
topic: simulation
summary: Simulation kinds, simulated time, fixed step, stability, determinism, fidelity and V&V for a training simulator.
tool: none
version: "n/a"
checked: 2026-11-12
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6, S7, S8, S9, S10, S11, S12, S13, S14, S15, S16, S17]

---

# Simulation principles

Contents: 1. Simulation kinds · 2. Simulated time · 3. Fixed step · 4. Integration and stability · 5. Determinism · 6. Fidelity · 7. Verification, validation, accreditation · 8. Pitfalls · Sources

## 1. Simulation kinds (discrete-event, continuous, hybrid)

A model describes a system. A simulation imitates the behavior of that system. [S14]

Three kinds of simulation exist.

- Discrete-event simulation: the state changes only at separate points of simulated time. Each change is an event. The clock jumps from one event to the next earliest event. Future events wait in a list sorted by time. [S1]
- Continuous simulation: the state changes all the time. A solver computes the continuous states by numerical integration, one step after the other. [S2]
- Hybrid simulation: both kinds in one program. A scheduler calls the continuous models at a fixed period and runs events between the steps. [S3] [S5]

A training simulator mixes both kinds. Physical processes are simulated continuously, in real time. Malfunctions and instructor commands occur at chosen times or on events. [S10]

Use these terms in code and comments. Use one term for one idea.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| model | The description of a system. [S14] |
| simulation | The imitation of the behavior of a system. [S14] |
| event | A happening that changes the state at one point of simulated time. [S1] |
| step | One advance of simulated time by the step size of the solver. [S2] |
| frame | One cycle of the scheduler. The scheduler compares simulated time with the wall clock at the end of each frame. [S4] |

## 2. Simulated time and wall-clock time (simulation time)

Simulated time is the time inside the simulation. Wall-clock time is the time of the host computer. They are two different clocks. [S3] [S5]

- The scheduler owns simulated time. Models receive it from the scheduler. [S3]
- Count simulated time as an integer number of ticks. A floating-point number of seconds has roundoff error at large values. [S3]
- Real time: at the end of each frame, the scheduler waits until the wall clock reaches simulated time. [S4]
- Underrun: the frame ends early and the scheduler waits. This is the normal case. [S4]
- Overrun: the frame takes longer than its period. The simulation runs slower than real time. The scheduler starts the next frame at once to catch up. [S4]
- Faster than real time: without the wait, the simulation runs as fast as the computer allows. [S4]
- Slow time and fast time: a training simulator can also run at a fraction or a multiple of real time. [S10]
- Freeze: simulated time stops. The program keeps running. Only work tied to the wall clock goes on. [S3] [S5]
- Save and restore: a saved state keeps simulated time. It does not keep wall-clock time. [S5]

Rule: a model never reads the wall clock. It uses only the simulated time and the step that the scheduler gives.

## 3. Fixed step and variable step (simulation time step)

A fixed-step solver uses the same step size from the start to the end of the run. A variable-step solver changes the step size during the run: smaller at an event or when the state changes fast, larger when the state changes slowly. [S2]

A real-time simulator uses a fixed step.

- Methods that change the time step do not suit real-time execution. [S11]
- Generated code that is deployed can use only a fixed-step solver. [S2]
- A fixed step gives the same result on every run. A step that varies does not. [S6]

A smaller step gives more accurate results and costs more computing time. [S2]

Wrong:

```csharp
// The step comes from the wall clock: it changes on every call.
double now = stopwatch.Elapsed.TotalSeconds;
model.Step(now - previous);
previous = now;
```

Right:

```csharp
// Fixed step. Simulated time is an integer count of ticks (1 tick = 1 microsecond).
const long StepTicks = 10_000;                      // 10 ms
const double StepSeconds = StepTicks / 1_000_000.0;

long simTicks = 0;
while (running)
{
    model.Step(StepSeconds);                 // always the same step
    simTicks += StepTicks;                   // integer time: no roundoff
    if (realTime)
    {
        realTimeClock.WaitUntil(simTicks);   // wait for the wall clock; skip to run faster
    }
}
```

The display rate can differ from the step rate. The display then interpolates between the last two states. [S6]

## 4. Integration and stability (simulation numerical integration)

A continuous model gives the derivatives of its states. An integrator computes the next states from them. Keep the two apart: one function for derivatives, one for integration. [S15]

- Common fixed-step integrators: Euler (1 derivative evaluation per step), Runge-Kutta 2 (2 evaluations), Runge-Kutta 4 (4 evaluations). More evaluations cost more time per step. [S15]
- Stability: with an explicit integrator, a step that is too large makes the computed values grow without bound. [S8]
- Stiff model: a model with a very fast part and a slow part. An explicit integrator then needs a very small step for the whole run. An implicit integrator stays stable with larger steps, but it solves an equation at each step. [S8]

Check the step size this way: [S9]

1. Run the simulation.
2. Run it again with a smaller step or a tighter tolerance.
3. Compare the two results.
4. If they do not differ significantly, the solution has converged. Keep the larger step.

Keep a step that passes this check and that the computer runs within its frame. [S4] [S9]

## 5. Determinism and reproducibility (simulation determinism)

A deterministic simulation gives the same outputs from the same initial state and the same inputs. Replay of recorded inputs then gives exactly the same result. [S7]

Follow these rules.

1. Use a fixed step. [S6]
2. Call the models in the same order on every step. [S4]
3. Never use wall-clock time in a model. Wall-clock time is not part of the saved state. [S3] [S5]
4. Never let the number of solver iterations depend on the speed of the processor. [S7]
5. Use stable numerical methods. An unstable method amplifies small differences. [S7]

Wrong:

```csharp
// Wall-clock time inside a model: different on every run, lost at restore.
pump.StartedAt = DateTime.UtcNow;
```

Right:

```csharp
// Simulated time only, given by the scheduler.
pump.StartedAtTicks = simTicks;
```

Limit: the same program on the same machine is reproducible. Another compiler, another processor architecture, or a debug build instead of a release build can change floating-point results. Do not expect bit-identical results across machines. [S7]

## 6. Fidelity and simulator classes (simulation fidelity)

Fidelity covers how specific to the real unit the simulator is, and how deep and accurate its models are. The training objectives set the level of fidelity that is needed. Higher fidelity costs more. [S10]

Simulators are named by their scope.

| Type | Scope |
| --- | --- |
| Full-scope simulator | Replica consoles and panels, and detailed models of the systems the operator works with. [S11] |
| Part-task simulator | One part of the operations or one phenomenon, often modeled more accurately. [S10] |
| Basic principle simulator | The fundamental physical processes. Auxiliary systems may be left out. [S10] |
| Plant analyser | Detailed study of complex transients. It need not run in real time. [S10] |

Maritime training simulators have classes: A (full mission), B (multi-task), C (limited task), S (special tasks). The standard is DNV-ST-0033. [S17]

Simulations are also named by who and what is real. [S16]

| Kind | People | Systems |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Live | Real | Real |
| Virtual | Real | Simulated |
| Constructive | Simulated | Simulated |

A training simulator with a real operator is a virtual simulation. [S16]

## 7. Verification, validation and accreditation (simulation VV&A)

- Verification: the program implements the model correctly. [S12] [S14]
- Validation: the results are accurate enough for the intended use. [S12] [S14]
- Accreditation: an official decision that the model is acceptable for a specific use. [S13]
- Credibility: the trust that users can put in the results. [S14]

State the accuracy that is required before you test. [S12]

These validation techniques work well as automated tests. [S13]

| Technique | What it checks |
| --- | --- |
| Comparison to other models | The results match a valid analytic model or a valid simulation model. |
| Degenerate test | The model behaves logically when a parameter takes an extreme value. |
| Extreme condition test | The outputs stay plausible for an unlikely combination of inputs. |
| Sensitivity analysis | The outputs respond to a change of input as the real system would. |
| Trace | One entity is followed through the model to check the logic. |

A training simulator is accepted by tests against reference data of the real plant. Each test has acceptance criteria, initial conditions and expected operator actions. The tests run in stages: before the factory test, at the factory, then on site. [S11]

## 8. Pitfalls (simulation principles)

- A job period that is not a whole number of ticks: the period is rounded and the job drifts. Example: a 128 Hz job with ticks of 1 microsecond has a period of 7812.5 ticks. Choose a tick that divides every period: with 10,000,000 ticks per second, the period is exactly 78,125 ticks. [S3]
- A frame overrun: the simulation falls behind real time. Set a limit on overruns and stop cleanly when it is passed. [S4]
- A loop that falls behind and runs more steps to catch up becomes slower again. Cap the number of steps per frame and accept a slower simulation. [S6]
- A step driven by the wall clock: results change from run to run, and a large step can make a model blow up. [S6]
- A stiff model with an explicit integrator: the values blow up, or the step must be very small. [S8]
- A method that changes the time step in a real-time loop: it does not suit real-time execution. [S11]
- Wall-clock time stored in the state: it is lost at restore. [S5]
- Bit-identical results expected across machines or builds: floating-point results can differ. [S7]

# Stimulation, emulation and hardware in the loop

Contents: 1. Four ways · 2. Constraints · Sources

## 1. Four ways to bring a real system into a simulator

| Way | Definition (paraphrase) |
| --- | --- |
| Stimulation | The real hardware and software of the plant run in the simulator, fed by the models [S1] |
| Emulation | The software of the real system is ported into the simulator environment and behaves as the reference [S1] |
| Simulation | Models written in the simulator environment reproduce the system within set functional limits [S1] |
| Hardware in the loop | A real embedded device is tested against a simulation of what it controls; emulated sensors and actuators make the interface [S2] |

## 2. Constraints of real or emulated systems

- A stimulated or emulated system must itself support freeze, run and backtrack, or a software layer must add them: a risk for functions and for the schedule. [S1]
- A hardware-in-the-loop bench needs a deterministic environment, with low latency and low jitter. [S2]
- In a virtualized full flight simulator, the motion computer keeps a hard real-time system and is not virtualized. [S3]
- Choose simulation when freeze, snapshot and replay matter more than an exact copy of the control software; choose stimulation or emulation when the training is about that software itself. Ask the team which one the project uses.

## Sources

- [S1] IAEA, "Guidelines for upgrade and modernization of nuclear power plant training simulators", IAEA-TECDOC-1500, 2006, <https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/Publications/PDF/te_1500_web.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Wikipedia, "Hardware-in-the-loop simulation", <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardware-in-the-loop_simulation>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY-SA 4.0, summarized.
- [S3] Arbic, Boivin, Giannias, "Leveraging Data Center Architectures for Full Flight Simulators", I/ITSEC 2024, paper 24155, <https://s3.amazonaws.com/amz.xcdsystem.com/44ECEE4F-033C-295C-BAE73278B7F9CA1D_abstract_File18333/StageTwoPaper_24155_0704044225.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# Real-time simulation in containers and Kubernetes

Contents: 1. Rule · 2. Published experience · 3. What to write · Sources

## 1. Rule of deployment (stepped core)

Keep the stepped core in one fixed-step process, as simple as possible; let out only data already computed. Split into services what surrounds it (instructor station, scenarios, snapshots, streaming), never the computation loop. Two published experiences go this way; none measured it on a .NET stack. [S3] [S5]

## 2. Published experience (containers and real time)

| Source | Context | Finding | Limit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NATO MSG-136, 2019 [S1] | "M&S as a Service": HLA federates in containers | The default Docker overlay network does not pass multicast; containers run unchanged under Docker and Kubernetes | No latency figure in the part read |
| Linutronix, 2023 [S2] | Real-time Linux kernel (PREEMPT_RT), cyclictest, one 4-core machine | Container latency almost equal to bare metal (about 23 µs), virtual machines higher (54 µs at most); needs extra Linux capabilities, the Guaranteed class and one physical core per real-time container | One machine; vendor blog |
| CAE, I/ITSEC 2024 [S3] | Virtualized full flight simulator, two containerized applications | Default settings: near 10 % of 100 Hz frames start 1 ms early or late; with exclusive cores, memory alignment, real-time kernel and fixed priority: as bare metal | Relaxed isolation; motion computer not virtualized; no Kubernetes |
| CAE, I/ITSEC 2025 [S4] | Distributed training on Kubernetes, Kafka, HLA and DIS gateways, Angular clients | Direct HLA about 3 ms; through messaging about 9 ms more; each gateway about 2 ms; almost every integration needed field-by-field fixes | Vendor measures |
| Kratzke, Siegfried, 2021 [S5] | Cloud-native simulation | Smaller composable services gain; moving an existing model as it is can raise costs | Read from the abstract |

## 3. What to write for the stepped core (Kubernetes)

- A Pod of class "Guaranteed": requests equal to limits, CPU as a whole number; the `static` CPU manager policy on the node gives it exclusive cores. [S6] See `docs/kubernetes/resources-realtime.md`.
- No multicast across the default container network: use host networking for that Pod or a unicast transport, by decision of the team. [S1]
- Measure frame overruns in the real cluster before any conclusion.

## Sources

- [S1] NATO STO MSG-136, "Modelling and Simulation as a Service, Volume 4: Experimentation Report", 2019, <https://apps.dtic.mil/sti/pdfs/AD1079102.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] Linutronix, "Containers and Realtime, is that possible? Even with Kubernetes?", 2023-07-06, <https://www.linutronix.de/blog/Containers-and-Realtime-is-that-possible-Even-with-Kubernetes>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] Arbic, Boivin, Giannias, "Leveraging Data Center Architectures for Full Flight Simulators", I/ITSEC 2024, <https://s3.amazonaws.com/amz.xcdsystem.com/44ECEE4F-033C-295C-BAE73278B7F9CA1D_abstract_File18333/StageTwoPaper_24155_0704044225.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] Krupp, Koch, Labas, Freeman, Bisaccia, "Achieving Distributed Training Through MSaaS", I/ITSEC 2025, <https://s3.amazonaws.com/amz.xcdsystem.com/44ECEE4F-033C-295C-BAE73278B7F9CA1D_abstract_File20450/PaperStage3FinalEdits_25271_0829031500.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S5] Kratzke, Siegfried, "Towards cloud-native simulations", Journal of Defense Modeling and Simulation 18(1), 2021, <https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:sae:joudef:v:18:y:2021:i:1:p:39-58>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S6] Kubernetes, "Control CPU Management Policies on the Node", <https://kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/administer-cluster/cpu-management-policies/>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.

# Training simulator architecture

Contents: 1. The parts · 2. Three references · 3. Order in a frame · Sources

## 1. The parts of a training simulator

The instructor station commands the executive; the executive calls the models; everything that shows, records or links hardware goes through the shared data.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  IOS["Instructor station"] -->|"freeze, run, scenario, malfunctions"| EXE["Executive: scheduler and simulated time"]
  EXE -->|"calls at each step"| MOD["Component models"]
  MOD -->|"outputs"| BUS["Shared data"]
  BUS -->|"inputs"| MOD
  EXE -->|"save and restore"| SNAP["State snapshots"]
  BUS --> REC["Recorder and replay"]
  BUS --> GW["Streaming gateway"]
  GW --> HMI["Web interface: synoptics"]
  HMI -->|"operator actions"| BUS
```

- The "data bus" is a table of named variables: written by the models, read by the instructor station, the consoles, the recorder and the gateways. Three references describe it under three names. [S1] [S2] [S4]
- The web interface reads published data; it never calls a model.

## 2. Three references for the same parts (IAEA, ECSS SMP, NASA Trick)

| Part | Nuclear training (IAEA) | Space (ECSS SMP) | NASA Trick |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Core | Operating system and simulator executive; their scan and transfer rates size the I/O [S1] | Simulation environment and its services: logger, time keeper, scheduler, event manager, resolver, link registry [S2] | Executive and scheduler; real-time sync apart [S3] |
| Models | Models of the plant systems [S1] | Components that implement the model interface, in a tree [S2] | C/C++ functions grouped in simulation objects, each with a job class [S4] |
| Shared data | Model variables, open to debug tools [S1] | Published fields with state, input and output flags [S2] | Variable server for network clients; data recording [S4] |
| Instructor station | Instructor station software [S1] | Outside the part read | Control panel, a client of the variable server [S4] |
| Life cycle | — | Building, connecting, initialising, standby, executing, storing, restoring, reconnecting, exiting, aborting [S2] | Initialisation, run, freeze, shutdown [S3] |

## 3. Order of calls in a frame (Trick)

Trick calls its job classes in a fixed order in each frame: top of frame, integration loop, environment, sensors, scheduled jobs, effectors, logging, time advance, end of frame. [S3] [S4]

Keep one fixed order in the simulator too: inputs (operator and instructor), models, outputs (shared data, recorder, gateways).

## Sources

- [S1] IAEA, "Guidelines for upgrade and modernization of nuclear power plant training simulators", IAEA-TECDOC-1500, 2006, <https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/Publications/PDF/te_1500_web.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] ECSS, "Simulation modelling platform", ECSS-E-ST-40-07C Rev.1, 2025-08-05, <https://ecss.nl/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/ECSS-E-ST-40-07C-Rev.1(5August2025).pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] NASA, Trick documentation, "Executive Scheduler", <https://nasa.github.io/trick/documentation/simulation_capabilities/Executive-Scheduler>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] Penn, Lin, "The Trick Simulation Toolkit", NASA NTRS 20150021786, <https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20150021786/downloads/20150021786.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# Simulation time and the scheduler loop

Contents: 1. Rules · 2. Ticks and rates · 3. The loop · 4. Overrun, freeze and time scale · Sources

## 1. Rules of simulation time

- A model advances only by its step. It never reads the clock of the machine. [S2] [S5]
- The step is fixed and comes from the scheduler. [S1] [S3]
- The executive tracks only simulated time; a separate part ties it to the wall clock for real time. [S2]
- Without that tie, the simulation runs as fast as it can. [S3]
- Four times exist: simulation time, epoch time, mission time and the wall clock ("Zulu"); the wall clock is never saved nor restored. [S5]

## 2. Ticks and rates (simulated time in integers)

- Count simulated time as an integer number of ticks, not as floating-point seconds: a double loses precision as the value grows. [S2]
- Every task period must be a whole number of ticks, else it is rounded and drifts: 128 Hz is 7,812.5 ticks of one microsecond, but exactly 78,125 ticks with 10 million ticks per second. [S2]
- Choose the tick so that every rate of the simulator divides it.

## 3. The scheduler loop (fixed step)

Advance only by fixed steps; keep the rest of the elapsed time in an accumulator; cap the steps per frame against the "spiral of death"; interpolate the display between the two last states. [S1]

```csharp
public sealed class Scheduler(IReadOnlyList<IModel> models, long stepTicks, TimeProvider clock)
{
    private long simulatedTicks;
    private long accumulatorTicks;
    private const int MaxStepsPerFrame = 5;

    public long SimulatedTicks => simulatedTicks;
    public bool IsFrozen { get; set; }
    public double TimeScale { get; set; } = 1.0;

    public void RunFrame(TimeSpan elapsed)
    {
        if (IsFrozen)
        {
            return;
        }
        accumulatorTicks += (long)(elapsed.Ticks * TimeScale);
        int steps = 0;
        while (accumulatorTicks >= stepTicks && steps < MaxStepsPerFrame)
        {
            foreach (IModel model in models)
            {
                model.Step(stepTicks);
            }
            simulatedTicks += stepTicks;
            accumulatorTicks -= stepTicks;
            steps++;
        }
    }
}
```

- The models are called in a fixed order at every step; a changing order breaks determinism. [S3]
- `TimeProvider` is the clock of the loop only; models never see it. The tick of this example is the .NET tick (100 ns).

## 4. Overrun, freeze and time scale (scheduler)

- A frame that ends early waits: the normal case. A frame that ends late (overrun) puts the simulation behind real time and the next frame starts at once; past a limit, stop cleanly. [S3]
- Freeze stops simulated time while the program runs: models are not called, the display stays alive. [S2] [S4]
- The instructor station changes the time scale: slow time (about a tenth of real time), fast time, step by step. [S4]
- The .NET garbage collector suspends managed threads during a collection; a sustained low latency mode exists for a long but bounded period. Measure the frame time in the real process. [S6]

## Sources

- [S1] Glenn Fiedler, "Fix Your Timestep!", 2004-06-10, <https://gafferongames.com/post/fix_your_timestep/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] NASA, Trick documentation, "Executive Scheduler", <https://nasa.github.io/trick/documentation/simulation_capabilities/Executive-Scheduler>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] NASA, Trick documentation, "Realtime", <https://nasa.github.io/trick/documentation/simulation_capabilities/Realtime>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] IAEA, "Selection, specification, design and use of various nuclear power plant training simulators", IAEA-TECDOC-995, 1998, <https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/publications/PDF/te_995_prn.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S5] ECSS, "Simulation modelling platform", ECSS-E-ST-40-07C Rev.1, 2025-08-05, <https://ecss.nl/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/ECSS-E-ST-40-07C-Rev.1(5August2025).pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S6] Microsoft, "Latency modes" (garbage collection), updated 2021-09-15, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/standard/garbage-collection/latency>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: simulation-training-functions
title: Training functions of a simulator
topic: simulation
summary: Freeze, snapshot, restore, backtrack, replay, scenarios and malfunctions, their behavior and what the models must provide.
tool: none

checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4, S5]

variant: chat
---

# Training functions of a simulator

Contents: 1. Rules · 2. The functions · 3. The French word "reprise" · Sources

## 1. Rules for the code of training functions

- Every command of the instructor station goes through the executive, dated in simulated time.
- The whole state of a model is in serializable fields: nothing in a hidden variable. [S3] [S4]
- A malfunction or a forcing is data of the saved state, set by the instructor station, never a special case in the code. [S4]
- A model advances only when it is called: freeze needs nothing from it.

## 2. The functions (training simulator)

| Function | What it is | What the code must do |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Instructor station | The station from which the instructor commands the simulator; it can force inputs and outputs [S2] | Send commands to the executive, never to a model |
| Scenario | A list of operations, for example malfunctions, to train or examine [S2] | A list of events, dated or conditional |
| Initial conditions | A start state chosen by the instructor [S1] | Load a full state before the first step |
| Freeze, run | Simulated time stops while the program runs; Trick freezes at the end of a step or frame and keeps calling freeze jobs; ECSS runs only the entry points tied to the wall clock [S5] [S1] [S4] | Stop calling the models; keep the display alive |
| Snapshot, checkpoint | A saved state; Trick writes a readable text file, at a time, at the end or periodically; ECSS makes persistence mandatory for a model [S3] [S1] [S4] | Save and restore every field of every model |
| Backtrack | Automatic snapshots at a set interval, for example 30 to 600 s [S1] | A snapshot must be fast and never delay the frame |
| Replay | Replays from a backtrack point, without operator action [S1] | Replay recorded inputs on a deterministic simulator, or read recorded outputs |
| Malfunction | Triggered on demand, at a time or on an event, as a step or a ramp [S1] | A named fault value in the state of the model |
| Failure, forcing | ECSS: a persistent failure object; a model can declare itself failable; a field can be forcible [S4] | Failures and forcings are saved with the state |
| Remote functions | Local plant actions commanded from the instructor station [S1] | The same interface as operator actions |
| Slow and fast time | About a tenth of real time, or faster [S1] | A ratio set in the executive, never in a model |

## 3. "Reprise" means two things in French (freeze and restore)

"Reprise" names both the restart after a freeze and the restore of a saved state. In English and in code, keep two words: `Run` (or `Resume`) and `Restore`.

## Sources

- [S1] IAEA, "Selection, specification, design and use of various nuclear power plant training simulators", IAEA-TECDOC-995, 1998, <https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/publications/PDF/te_995_prn.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] IAEA, "Guidelines for upgrade and modernization of nuclear power plant training simulators", IAEA-TECDOC-1500, 2006, <https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/Publications/PDF/te_1500_web.pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S3] NASA, Trick documentation, "Checkpoints", <https://nasa.github.io/trick/documentation/simulation_capabilities/Checkpoints>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] ECSS, "Simulation modelling platform", ECSS-E-ST-40-07C Rev.1, 2025-08-05, <https://ecss.nl/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/ECSS-E-ST-40-07C-Rev.1(5August2025).pdf>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S5] NASA, Trick documentation, "Executive Scheduler", <https://nasa.github.io/trick/documentation/simulation_capabilities/Executive-Scheduler>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

# French to English simulation vocabulary

Contents: 1. How to use · 2. Terms A to L · 3. Terms M to Z · Sources

## 1. How to use the simulation vocabulary

- Find the French term in the first column, lowercase. Use the English term in code, names and answers.
- "Reprise" names both the run after a freeze and the restore of a saved state: in English and in code, keep `Run` and `Restore` apart.
- Several French terms follow the usage of the team, not a published definition: check them against the internal glossary. [S1] [S2] [S3] [S4]

# Simulation data to a web interface

Contents: 1. Three zones · 2. Documented means · 3. Rules for the gateway · Sources

## 1. Three zones (simulator and web interface)

Only zone 1 is held to the fixed step and to determinism; zones 2 and 3 are ordinary services.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
  subgraph Z1["Zone 1: stepped core, deterministic"]
    EXE["Fixed-step executive"] --> MOD["Models"]
  end
  subgraph Z2["Zone 2: services"]
    API["Instructor station, scenarios, snapshots"]
  end
  subgraph Z3["Zone 3: web streaming"]
    GW["Gateway: subscriptions, reduced rate"] --> UI["Browser: synoptics"]
  end
  MOD -->|"state published at each step"| GW
  API -->|"commands dated in simulated time"| EXE
  UI -->|"actions"| API
```

## 2. Documented means (push, history, rate)

| Need | Means | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Push data from the server to the browser | SignalR: WebSockets first, then Server-Sent Events, then long polling; JSON or MessagePack (binary, smaller messages); advised for frequent updates, dashboards and monitoring | [S1] |
| Keep real time apart from history | Open MCT: a subscription delivers each new value; a request returns a past range, sorted in time | [S2] [S3] |
| Send no more than the screen shows | Open MCT: a request strategy that returns only the minimum and maximum per pixel | [S3] |
| Show at another rate than the computation | Interpolate between the two last states | [S4] |
| Signal limits | Open MCT: limit evaluators with five levels | [S3] |
| Link a web application to DIS or HLA | WebLVC, being standardized by SISO in 2016, through a message protocol and a gateway server; current status not checked | [S5] |

## 3. Rules for the gateway (synoptic streaming)

- The web interface reads published data; it never calls a model.
- Send the synoptics a state reduced to the screen rate (a few frames per second), not every computation step; on connection, a full state, then only the changes. Tune the rates by trial: no source gives a figure for a synoptic.
- Send operator actions to the services of zone 2, which date them in simulated time for the executive.
- With several gateway replicas, SignalR needs sticky sessions, or WebSockets only with negotiation skipped (`docs/architecture/` when present).

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft, "Overview of ASP.NET Core SignalR", ASP.NET Core 10.0, <https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/signalr/introduction>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S2] NASA, "Open MCT", README, <https://github.com/nasa/openmct>, read 2026-11-12, Apache 2.0, summarized.
- [S3] NASA, "Open MCT, API.md", <https://github.com/nasa/openmct/blob/master/API.md>, read 2026-11-12, Apache 2.0, summarized.
- [S4] Glenn Fiedler, "Fix Your Timestep!", 2004-06-10, <https://gafferongames.com/post/fix_your_timestep/>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S5] FFI, "Web Live, Virtual and Constructive (WebLVC)", report 16/01189, 2016, <https://www.ffi.no/en/publications-archive/web-live-virtual-and-constructive-weblvc>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: typescript-lint-angular-components-v22
title: Angular 22 components
topic: typescript-lint
summary: Rules for inject(), signal inputs and outputs, lifecycle methods, host bindings and standalone components. Angular 22.
tool: angular-eslint
version: "22.6"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2]

# Angular 22 components

Contents: 1. inject() · 2. Signal inputs · 3. Outputs · 4. Lifecycle methods · 5. Host bindings · 6. Output names · 7. Standalone · Sources

In Angular 22, components are standalone and use OnPush by default: never write `standalone: true`
and never write `changeDetection` unless the project asks for it. [S2]

## 1. Inject with inject(), not with the constructor (angular-eslint prefer-inject)

Get a service with a field: `private readonly api = inject(ValveApi);`. Never list services in the constructor. [S1]

Wrong:

```ts
@Injectable({ providedIn: 'root' })
export class PumpStore {
  public constructor(private readonly http: HttpClient) {}
}
```

Right, with `@Service()`, the service decorator of Angular 22:

```ts
@Service()
export class PumpStore {
  private readonly http = inject(HttpClient);

  public load(): Observable<unknown> {
    return this.http.get<unknown>('/api/pumps');
  }
}
```

## 2. Use signal inputs (angular-eslint prefer-signals)

Declare an input with `input()` or `input.required()`, never with `@Input()`. Read it as a function in the template: `label()`. [S1]

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-pump-label', template: '{{ label }}' })
export class PumpLabel {
  @Input() public label = '';
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-pump-label', template: '{{ label() }}' })
export class PumpLabel {
  public readonly label = input('');
}
```

## 3. Use output() (angular-eslint prefer-output-emitter-ref)

Declare an output with `output<Type>()`, never with `@Output()` and `EventEmitter`. [S1]

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({
  selector: 'app-stop-button',
  template: '<button type="button" (click)="stopped.emit()">Stop</button>',
})
export class StopButton {
  @Output() public readonly stopped = new EventEmitter<void>();
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({
  selector: 'app-stop-button',
  template: '<button type="button" (click)="stopped.emit()">Stop</button>',
})
export class StopButton {
  public readonly stopped = output();
}
```

## 4. Keep lifecycle methods synchronous (angular-eslint no-async-lifecycle-method)

Never write `async ngOnInit()`. Angular does not wait for it. Start the work with `void`, in a separate method. [S1]

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-valve-count', template: '{{ count() }}' })
export class ValveCount implements OnInit {
  protected readonly count = signal(0);
  private readonly api = inject(ValveApi);

  public async ngOnInit(): Promise<void> {
    this.count.set((await this.api.loadValves()).length);
  }
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-valve-count', template: '{{ count() }}' })
export class ValveCount implements OnInit {
  protected readonly count = signal(0);
  private readonly api = inject(ValveApi);

  public ngOnInit(): void {
    void this.load();
  }

  private async load(): Promise<void> {
    const valves = await this.api.loadValves();

    this.count.set(valves.length);
  }
}
```

## 5. Bind host events in the metadata (angular-eslint prefer-host-metadata-property)

Write host events and bindings in `host: { … }` of `@Component`, never with `@HostListener` or `@HostBinding`. [S1]

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-escape-stop', template: '' })
export class EscapeStop {
  public readonly stopped = output();

  @HostListener('window:keydown.escape')
  protected stop(): void {
    this.stopped.emit();
  }
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({
  selector: 'app-escape-stop',
  template: '',
  host: { '(window:keydown.escape)': 'stop()' },
})
export class EscapeStop {
  public readonly stopped = output();

  protected stop(): void {
    this.stopped.emit();
  }
}
```

## 6. Name an output after the event, without "on" (angular-eslint no-output-on-prefix)

Name an output `stopped` or `valveSelected`, never `onStop`. [S1]

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-pump-stop', template: '' })
export class PumpStop {
  public readonly onStop = output();
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-pump-stop', template: '' })
export class PumpStop {
  public readonly stopped = output();
}
```

## 7. Keep components standalone (angular-eslint prefer-standalone)

Never write `standalone: false`. Import what the template needs in `imports: [ … ]` of the component. [S1] [S2]

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-tank-view', standalone: false, template: '' })
export class TankView {}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-tank-view', template: '' })
export class TankView {}
```

## Sources

- [S1] angular-eslint, rules of @angular-eslint/eslint-plugin, version 22.6, <https://github.com/angular-eslint/angular-eslint/tree/main/packages/eslint-plugin/docs/rules>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S2] Google, "Angular coding style guide" and "Components", Angular 22, <https://angular.dev/style-guide>, read 2026-11-13, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
=====BASE-EXPORT-1-eccf69c80ed6===== END
=====BASE-EXPORT-1-eccf69c80ed6===== FILE 1/1 b39ea53a56060ecb2bfdf32a419a8e9e2d60a4a6cf3c98c507020b4716998cb3 RAW chat/knowledge/typescript-lint/angular-rxjs.md
---
id: typescript-lint-angular-rxjs
title: Angular and RxJS
topic: typescript-lint
summary: Rules to end subscriptions with the component and to turn streams into signals. Angular 22, RxJS 7.
tool: angular-eslint
version: "22.6"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2]
license: MIT, examples written for this base
variant: chat
---

# Angular and RxJS

Contents: 1. Streams as signals · 2. takeUntilDestroyed · 3. Pitfalls · Sources

## 1. Turn a stream into a signal (angular-eslint no-implicit-take-until-destroyed)

To show the last value of a stream, use `toSignal()` in a field. It ends the subscription with the component. [S2]

Why: `takeUntilDestroyed()` without an argument works only during the creation of the component. Called in `ngOnInit` or in a method, it throws an error.

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-alarm-banner', template: '{{ last() }}' })
export class AlarmBanner implements OnInit {
  protected readonly last = signal('');
  private readonly alarms = inject(AlarmFeed);

  public ngOnInit(): void {
    this.alarms.messages$.pipe(takeUntilDestroyed()).subscribe((message) => {
      this.last.set(message);
    });
  }
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-alarm-banner', template: '{{ last() }}' })
export class AlarmBanner {
  private readonly alarms = inject(AlarmFeed);
  protected readonly last = toSignal(this.alarms.messages$, { initialValue: '' });
}
```

## 2. Subscribe in a field when you must (angular-eslint no-implicit-take-until-destroyed)

When a stream must run code, subscribe while the component is created: in a field or the constructor, with `takeUntilDestroyed()`. [S1] [S2]

Wrong:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-alarm-sound', template: '' })
export class AlarmSound {
  private readonly alarms = inject(AlarmFeed);

  public start(): void {
    this.alarms.messages$.pipe(takeUntilDestroyed()).subscribe(() => {
      this.ring();
    });
  }

  private ring(): void {
    this.rang.set(true);
  }

  private readonly rang = signal(false);
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({ selector: 'app-alarm-sound', template: '' })
export class AlarmSound {
  private readonly alarms = inject(AlarmFeed);
  private readonly rang = signal(false);

  public constructor() {
    this.alarms.messages$.pipe(takeUntilDestroyed()).subscribe(() => {
      this.rang.set(true);
    });
  }
}
```

## 3. Pitfalls (RxJS in Angular)

- A `subscribe()` inside another `subscribe()`: no lint rule catches it. Chain the streams with `switchMap` instead.
- `toPromise()` is deprecated in RxJS 7: use `firstValueFrom()` or `lastValueFrom()`.
- Keep state in signals; keep streams for events that happen over time (messages, timers, sockets).

## Sources

- [S1] angular-eslint, "no-implicit-take-until-destroyed", version 22.6, <https://github.com/angular-eslint/angular-eslint/blob/main/packages/eslint-plugin/docs/rules/no-implicit-take-until-destroyed.md>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S2] Google, "RxJS interop with Angular signals", Angular 22, <https://angular.dev/ecosystem/rxjs-interop>, read 2026-11-13, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
=====BASE-EXPORT-1-eccf69c80ed6===== END

---
id: typescript-lint-angular-templates-v22
title: Angular 22 templates
topic: typescript-lint
summary: Rules for control flow, class bindings, buttons, keyboard access and empty lists in templates. Angular 22.
tool: angular-eslint
version: "22.6"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2]
license: MIT, examples written for this base
variant: chat

# Angular 22 templates

Contents: 1. Control flow · 2. Class bindings · 3. Button type · 4. Click and keyboard · 5. Empty lists · Sources

## 1. Use @if and @for, never *ngIf and *ngFor (angular-eslint template prefer-control-flow)

Write `@if`, `@for` and `@switch` blocks. `*ngIf`, `*ngFor` and `*ngSwitch` are old. Each `@for` needs a `track` expression. [S1] [S2]

Wrong:

```html
<ul>
  <li *ngFor="let valve of valves">{{ valve.label }}</li>
</ul>
```

Right:

```html
<ul>
  @for (valve of valves(); track valve.id) {
    <li>{{ valve.label }}</li>
  }
</ul>
```

## 2. Bind one class at a time (angular-eslint template prefer-class-binding)

Write `[class.fault]="isFault()"`, never `[ngClass]`. Write `[style.width.px]`, never `[ngStyle]`. [S1]

Wrong:

```html
<li [ngClass]="{ fault: isFault() }">Valve</li>
```

Right:

```html
<li [class.fault]="isFault()">Valve</li>
```

## 3. Give every button its type (angular-eslint template button-has-type)

Write `type="button"` on each button that is not the submit button of a form. [S1]

Why: a button without type submits the form around it.

Wrong:

```html
<button (click)="stop()">Stop</button>
```

Right:

```html
<button type="button" (click)="stop()">Stop</button>
```

## 4. Put a click on a button, never on a plain element (angular-eslint template click-events-have-key-events)

Put `(click)` on a `<button>` or a link. A click on a `<li>` or a `<div>` cannot be reached with the keyboard. [S1]

Wrong:

```html
<li (click)="select()">Valve V-101</li>
```

Right:

```html
<li>
  <button type="button" (click)="select()">Valve V-101</button>
</li>
```

## 5. Show an empty list with @empty (angular-eslint template prefer-at-empty)

For the text of an empty list, write an `@empty` block inside `@for`, not a second `@if`. [S1]

Wrong:

```html
@for (valve of valves(); track valve.id) {
  <p>{{ valve.label }}</p>
}
@if (valves().length === 0) {
  <p>No valve</p>
}
```

Right:

```html
@for (valve of valves(); track valve.id) {
  <p>{{ valve.label }}</p>
} @empty {
  <p>No valve</p>
}
```

## Sources

- [S1] angular-eslint, rules of @angular-eslint/eslint-plugin-template, version 22.6, <https://github.com/angular-eslint/angular-eslint/tree/main/packages/eslint-plugin-template/docs/rules>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S2] Google, "Control flow", Angular 22, <https://angular.dev/guide/templates/control-flow>, read 2026-11-13, CC BY 4.0, summarized.

---
id: typescript-lint-ts-angular-strict-format
title: Strict formatting
topic: typescript-lint
summary: Member order to write by hand, and what the fix command orders for you. ESLint 10, STRICT_FORMAT.
tool: eslint
version: "10.14"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2]
license: MIT, examples written for this base
variant: chat
---

# Strict formatting

Contents: 1. Member order · 2. What the fix command does · 3. The switch · Sources

## 1. Write fields, then the constructor, then methods (typescript-eslint member-ordering)

In a class, write all fields first, then the constructor, then the methods. The fix command cannot move them for you. [S1]

Wrong:

```ts
export class TankGauge {
  public percent(level: number): number {
    return (level / this.capacity) * 100;
  }

  private readonly capacity = 40;
}
```

Right:

```ts
export class TankGauge {
  private readonly capacity = 40;

  public percent(level: number): number {
    return (level / this.capacity) * 100;
  }
}
```

## 2. What the fix command orders for you (ESLint strict formatting)

`npm run fix` sorts imports and exports, adds braces to every `if`, adds a blank line before each `return` and between class members, and sorts the keys of Angular decorators. Prettier then sets spaces and line breaks. Never do these by hand: run the command. [S2]

## 3. The switch (STRICT_FORMAT)

The strict rules are on by default. `STRICT_FORMAT=0 npm run check` runs the check without them. Never turn them off to hide an error.

## Sources

- [S1] typescript-eslint, "member-ordering", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/member-ordering>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S2] Rules of `eslint.strict-format.mjs` in `templates/typescript-angular/` of this base: ESLint core, `@stylistic/eslint-plugin` 5.10, `eslint-plugin-simple-import-sort` 14, angular-eslint 22.6, all MIT.

=====BASE-EXPORT-1-eccf69c80ed6===== FILE 1/1 75ed0d2af159e485cf031d6e16b80d6fc2257e7eacad27274309d811127f8ac9 RAW chat/knowledge/typescript-lint/ts-async.md
---
id: typescript-lint-ts-async
title: TypeScript promises
topic: typescript-lint
summary: Rules to handle every promise, await before a test, and use async only with await. typescript-eslint 8.
tool: typescript-eslint
version: "8.72"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3]
license: MIT, examples written for this base
variant: chat
---

# TypeScript promises

Contents: 1. Handle every promise · 2. Await before you test · 3. async only with await · 4. Pitfalls · Sources

## 1. Handle every promise (typescript-eslint no-floating-promises)

Handle each promise: `await` it, `return` it, or mark it with `void` when its result does not matter. [S1]

Why: a promise that nobody handles hides its error.

Wrong:

```ts
export class ValveSaver {
  private readonly router = inject(Router);

  public save(): void {
    this.router.navigate(['/valves']);
  }
}
```

Right:

```ts
export class ValveSaver {
  private readonly router = inject(Router);

  public async save(): Promise<void> {
    await this.router.navigate(['/valves']);
  }
}
```

For several promises, join them with `Promise.all()`, then handle the result. [S1]

## 2. Await a promise before you test it (typescript-eslint no-misused-promises)

Never put a promise in a condition. Await it first. [S2]

Why: a promise is always truthy, so the test is always true.

Wrong:

```ts
export class ValveCloser {
  private readonly api = inject(ValveApi);

  public async closeIfOpen(id: string): Promise<void> {
    if (this.api.isOpen(id)) {
      await this.api.close(id);
    }
  }
}
```

Right:

```ts
export class ValveCloser {
  private readonly api = inject(ValveApi);

  public async closeIfOpen(id: string): Promise<void> {
    if (await this.api.isOpen(id)) {
      await this.api.close(id);
    }
  }
}
```

## 3. Use async only with await (typescript-eslint require-await)

Mark a function `async` only when it contains `await`. [S3]

Why: an `async` function without `await` wraps a plain value in a promise for nothing.

Wrong:

```ts
export async function valveLabel(valve: Valve): Promise<string> {
  return valve.label;
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function valveLabel(valve: Valve): string {
  return valve.label;
}
```

## 4. Pitfalls (typescript-eslint promise rules)

- `void` does not handle the error of the promise. Use `void` only when the error does not matter. Else use `await` inside `try`. [S1]
- An event handler of a template that calls an `async` method: write `void this.save()` in a method that returns `void`.
- Never add a comment to switch a promise rule off: such comments have no effect in this project.

## Sources

- [S1] typescript-eslint, "no-floating-promises", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-floating-promises>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S2] typescript-eslint, "no-misused-promises", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-misused-promises>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S3] typescript-eslint, "require-await", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/require-await>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.

title: TypeScript null checks and conditions
topic: typescript-lint
summary: Rules for index access, needless conditions, complete switch statements, template strings and nullish values. TypeScript 6.0.
tool: typescript-eslint, TypeScript

# TypeScript null checks and conditions

Contents: 1. Index access · 2. Needless conditions · 3. Complete switch · 4. Template strings · 5. Nullish values · Sources

## 1. A value read by its index can be undefined (TypeScript noUncheckedIndexedAccess)

With `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, `list[0]` has the type `Item | undefined`. Check it before you use it. [S1]

Why: an empty array or a missing key gives `undefined`, not an item.

Wrong:

```ts
export function firstId(valves: readonly Valve[]): string {
  return valves[0].id;
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function firstId(valves: readonly Valve[]): string | undefined {
  return valves[0]?.id;
}
```

## 2. Test only what can change (typescript-eslint no-unnecessary-condition)

Never test a value that the types say is always set, or always true. Fix the type instead. [S2]

Why: a needless test hides a wrong type, and the other branch is dead code.

Wrong:

```ts
export function describeValve(valve: Valve): string {
  if (valve !== undefined) {
    return valve.label;
  }

  return 'unknown';
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function describeValve(valve: Valve | undefined): string {
  return valve?.label ?? 'unknown';
}
```

## 3. Handle every case of a union in a switch (typescript-eslint switch-exhaustiveness-check)

A `switch` on a union type has one `case` per member. Add no `default` that hides a new member. [S3]

Why: when a new state is added, the compiler then shows every switch to update.

Wrong:

```ts
export function stateText(state: ValveState): string {
  switch (state) {
    case 'open':
      return 'Open';
    case 'closed':
      return 'Closed';
    default:
      return '';
  }
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function stateText(state: ValveState): string {
  switch (state) {
    case 'open':
      return 'Open';
    case 'closed':
      return 'Closed';
    case 'fault':
      return 'Fault';
  }
}
```

## 4. Put only strings and numbers in a template string (typescript-eslint restrict-template-expressions)

In `${…}`, put a string or a number. Give a fallback for a value that can be `undefined`. [S4]

Why: `${undefined}` prints the word "undefined" on the screen.

Wrong:

```ts
export function tankLine(tank: string | undefined, percent: number): string {
  return `${tank} ${percent} %`;
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function tankLine(tank: string | undefined, percent: number): string {
  return `${tank ?? 'unknown tank'} ${percent} %`;
}
```

## 5. Use ?? for a missing value (typescript-eslint prefer-nullish-coalescing)

Use `??` to replace `undefined` or `null`. Use `||` only to replace every false value on purpose. [S5]

Why: `||` also replaces `0` and the empty string, which are real values.

Wrong:

```ts
export function labelOf(label: string | undefined): string {
  return label || 'none';
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function labelOf(label: string | undefined): string {
  return label ?? 'none';
}
```

## Sources

- [S1] Microsoft, "TSConfig Reference: noUncheckedIndexedAccess", TypeScript 6.0, <https://www.typescriptlang.org/tsconfig/#noUncheckedIndexedAccess>, read 2026-11-13, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] typescript-eslint, "no-unnecessary-condition", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-unnecessary-condition>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S3] typescript-eslint, "switch-exhaustiveness-check", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/switch-exhaustiveness-check>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S4] typescript-eslint, "restrict-template-expressions", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/restrict-template-expressions>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S5] typescript-eslint, "prefer-nullish-coalescing", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/prefer-nullish-coalescing>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.

id: typescript-lint-ts-types
title: TypeScript types without any
topic: typescript-lint
summary: Rules for any, unknown, type assertions, non-null assertions and thrown values, with examples. typescript-eslint 8.
tool: typescript-eslint
version: "8.72"
checked: 2026-11-13

# TypeScript types without any

Contents: 1. unknown, never any · 2. Check the shape · 3. Never write ! · 4. Throw an Error · Pitfalls · Sources

## 1. Use unknown, never any (typescript-eslint no-explicit-any)

Never write `any`. Write `unknown`, then check the value before you use it. [S1]

Why: `any` turns the type checks off, so every later error goes unseen.

Wrong:

```ts
export function readLevel(sample: any): number {
  return sample.level;
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function readLevel(sample: unknown): number {
  if (typeof sample === 'object' && sample !== null && 'level' in sample) {
    return Number(sample.level);
  }

  return 0;
}
```

## 2. Check the shape, never force a type (typescript-eslint no-unsafe-type-assertion)

Never write `as <Type>` to give a value a narrower type. Check its shape with a type guard. [S2]

Why: `as` tells the compiler to trust you. A wrong shape then breaks at run time, far away.

Wrong:

```ts
export function toValves(body: unknown): Valve[] {
  return body as Valve[];
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function toValves(body: unknown): Valve[] {
  if (!Array.isArray(body) || !body.every(isValve)) {
    throw new Error('The valve list has a wrong shape.');
  }

  return body;
}
```

## 3. Check for undefined, never write ! (typescript-eslint no-non-null-assertion)

Never write `!` after a value. Use `?.` and `??`, or test for `undefined`. [S3]

Why: `!` hides a value that can be missing. The code then fails on the first missing value.

Wrong:

```ts
export function firstOpenLabel(valves: readonly Valve[]): string {
  return valves.find((valve) => valve.state === 'open')!.label;
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function firstOpenLabel(valves: readonly Valve[]): string {
  return valves.find((valve) => valve.state === 'open')?.label ?? 'none';
}
```

## 4. Throw an Error object (typescript-eslint only-throw-error)

Throw only `Error` objects: `throw new Error('message')`. [S4]

Why: a thrown string has no stack trace, and `catch` code expects an `Error`.

Wrong:

```ts
export function requireOpen(valve: Valve): void {
  if (valve.state !== 'open') {
    throw 'The valve is closed.';
  }
}
```

Right:

```ts
export function requireOpen(valve: Valve): void {
  if (valve.state !== 'open') {
    throw new Error('The valve is closed.');
  }
}
```

## 5. Pitfalls (typescript-eslint unsafe rules)

- `Unsafe member access .x on an any value` or `Unsafe return of a value of type any`: an `any` came in earlier. Find where it came from and type it as `unknown` there.
- A value in `catch (error)` has the type `unknown`. Check it with `error instanceof Error` before you read `error.message`.
- `JSON.parse` and an HTTP body give an untyped value: read them as `unknown`, then check them.

## Sources

- [S1] typescript-eslint, "no-explicit-any", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-explicit-any>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S2] typescript-eslint, "no-unsafe-type-assertion", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-unsafe-type-assertion>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S3] typescript-eslint, "no-non-null-assertion", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/no-non-null-assertion>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.
- [S4] typescript-eslint, "only-throw-error", version 8, <https://typescript-eslint.io/rules/only-throw-error>, read 2026-11-13, MIT, summarized.

topic: angular

tool: angular
version: "22"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6, S7, S8, S9, S10, S11, S12, S13]

# Angular old patterns and their replacements

Contents: 1. Version rule · 2. Components and templates · 3. State, data and forms · 4. Services, HTTP and routing · 5. Tests and build · 6. Migration commands · Old patterns · Sources

## 1. Version rule (Angular old and new patterns)

Read the major version of `@angular/core` in `package.json`. Use a replacement only when that major version is equal to or higher than the `Since` column. Below it, write the old pattern. `17` in the column means every version this document covers. [S1]

Keep one style per file. In a file that already uses the old pattern, keep it, or migrate the whole file with a command of section 6.

Wrong, in a project with `"@angular/core": "^22.0.0"`:

```ts
@Component({
  selector: 'app-user-card',
  standalone: true,
  imports: [CommonModule],
  changeDetection: ChangeDetectionStrategy.OnPush,
  template: `<p *ngIf="user.active">{{ user.name }}</p>`,
})
export class UserCardComponent {
  @Input() user!: User;
  constructor(private readonly users: UserService) {}
}
```

Right:

```ts
@Component({
  selector: 'app-user-card',
  template: `
    @if (user().active) {
      <p>{{ user().name }}</p>
    }
  `,
})
export class UserCard {
  private readonly users = inject(UserService);
  public readonly user = input.required<User>();
}
```

## 2. Components and templates (Angular 17 to 22)

| Old pattern | Write instead | Since | Status of the old pattern |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `standalone: true` in the decorator | Nothing: standalone is the default | 19 | Redundant. A class declared in an NgModule needs `standalone: false`. [S5] |
| `@Input() name` | `readonly name = input<T>()` or `input.required<T>()` | 19 | Still works [S5] [S2] |
| `@Output() x = new EventEmitter<T>()` | `readonly x = output<T>()` | 19 | Still works [S5] [S2] |
| `@Input() v` with `@Output() vChange` | `readonly v = model<T>()` | 19 | Still works [S5] |
| `@ViewChild`, `@ContentChild` | `viewChild()`, `contentChild()` | 19 | Still works [S5] |
| `*ngIf`, `*ngFor`, `[ngSwitch]` | `@if`, `@for (item of items; track item.id)`, `@switch` | 18 | Deprecated in 20 [S6] |
| Template part loaded by hand | `@defer { … }` | 18 | — [S7] |
| `[ngClass]`, `[ngStyle]` | `[class.name]`, `[style.prop]`, `[class]`, `[style]` | 17 | Discouraged [S2] |
| `imports: [CommonModule]` | Import only the pipes and directives the template uses | 17 | Discouraged [S2] |
| `@HostBinding`, `@HostListener` | `host: { … }` in the decorator | 17 | Discouraged [S2] |
| `changeDetection: ChangeDetectionStrategy.OnPush` | Nothing: OnPush is the default | 22 | Redundant. `Default` is renamed `Eager`. [S4] |
| Constructor injection | `private readonly x = inject(X)` | 17 | Still works [S2] |
| `{{ this.item }}` to read a template variable | `{{ item }}` | 19 | Broken in 19 [S5] |

## 3. State, data and forms (Angular 17 to 22)

| Old pattern | Write instead | Since | Status of the old pattern |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Plain field or `BehaviorSubject` for component state | `signal()`, `computed()` | 17 | Still works [S2] |
| State derived from several sources, kept in sync by hand | `linkedSignal()` | 20 | — [S6] |
| `mySignal.mutate(…)` | `mySignal.update(…)` or `mySignal.set(…)` | 17 | Removed in 17 |
| `HttpClient` call, `subscribe`, loading flag | `resource({params, loader})` or `httpResource(() => url)` | 22 | Still works. Experimental before 22. [S3] [S10] |
| Reactive forms for a new form | Signal Forms: `form(model, schema)` and `[formField]` | 22 | Still supported. Experimental in 21. [S3] [S9] |
| `takeUntil(this.destroy$)` with `ngOnDestroy` | `takeUntilDestroyed()` | 19 | Still works [S5] |
| Manual `subscribe` to copy a value into a field | `toSignal(source$, {initialValue: …})` | 20 | The `async` pipe stays right in templates [S6] [S11] |
| `zone.js`, change detection driven by `NgZone` | `provideZonelessChangeDetection()` and signals | 21 | New applications have no `zone.js` from 21 [S7] |

Read a resource with `user.hasValue()`, `user.value()`, `user.isLoading()` and `user.error()`. [S10]

## 4. Services, HTTP and routing (Angular 17 to 22)

| Old pattern | Write instead | Since | Status of the old pattern |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `@Injectable({providedIn: 'root'})` | `@Service()` | 22 | Still works [S3] [S12] |
| `HttpClientModule` in `imports` | `provideHttpClient()` in `providers` | 18 | Deprecated in 18 |
| `provideHttpClient(withFetch())` | `provideHttpClient()` | 22 | `withFetch` deprecated in 22 [S4] |
| `ComponentFactoryResolver` | `viewContainerRef.createComponent(MyComponent)` | 17 | Removed in 22 [S4] |
| `provideRoutes(routes)` | `provideRouter(routes)` | 17 | Removed in 22 [S4] |
| `Router.errorHandler` | `withNavigationErrorHandler()` in `provideRouter()` | 19 | Removed in 19 [S5] |

## 5. Tests and build (Angular 17 to 22)

| Old pattern | Write instead | Since | Status of the old pattern |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `TestBed.get(X)` | `TestBed.inject(X)` | 17 | Removed in 20 [S6] |
| `async(() => …)` in a test | `waitForAsync(() => …)` | 17 | Removed in 18 |
| `TestBed.flushEffects()` | `TestBed.tick()` | 20 | Removed in 20 [S6] |
| `RouterTestingModule` | `provideRouter()` and `provideLocationMocks()` | 17 | Deprecated in 21 [S8] |
| Karma and Jasmine in a new project | Vitest, builder `@angular/build:unit-test` | 21 | Karma is still supported [S7] |
| Builders of `@angular-devkit/build-angular` (Webpack) | Builders of `@angular/build` | 18 | Deprecated in 22 |
| File `user-card.component.ts`, class `UserCardComponent` | File `user-card.ts`, class `UserCard` | 20 | Keep the naming the project already uses [S2] |

## 6. Migration commands (Angular schematics)

Do not rewrite an old pattern by hand across a project. Run the command from the project root. [S8] [S13]

| Change | Command |
| --- | --- |
| NgModules to standalone | `ng generate @angular/core:standalone-migration` |
| `*ngIf`, `*ngFor` to `@if`, `@for` | `ng generate @angular/core:control-flow-migration` |
| Constructor injection to `inject()` | `ng generate @angular/core:inject-migration` |
| `@Input` to `input()` | `ng generate @angular/core:signal-input-migration` |
| `@Output` to `output()` | `ng generate @angular/core:output-migration` |
| Query decorators to signal queries | `ng generate @angular/core:signal-queries-migration` |
| `ngClass` to `class` bindings | `ng generate @angular/core:ngclass-to-class-migration` |
| `CommonModule` to single imports | `ng generate @angular/core:common-to-standalone-migration` |
| `@Injectable` to `@Service`, from 22.1 | `ng generate @angular/core:service` |

- A command exists only from the version that introduced it. When it fails with an unknown schematic, show the error and stop. Do not rewrite the code by hand instead.
- Run the `inject-migration` before the `@Service` migration: it skips classes that inject through their constructor. [S12]
- To move to a new major version: `ng update @angular/core@<major> @angular/cli@<major>`, one major version at a time.

## Old patterns

- `standalone: true` (before 19), `*ngIf` and `*ngFor` (before 18), `changeDetection: ChangeDetectionStrategy.OnPush` (before 22): do not write them in new code of a project at these versions or later.
- `HttpClientModule` (before 18), `TestBed.get()` (before 20), `ComponentFactoryResolver` (before 22): do not use them.

## Sources

- [S1] Angular Team, "Version compatibility", v22, <https://angular.dev/reference/versions>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S2] Angular Team, "LLM prompts and AI IDE setup" and "Angular coding style guide", v22, <https://angular.dev/ai/develop-with-ai>, read 2026-11-12, CC BY 4.0, summarized.
- [S3] Angular Team, "Announcing Angular v22", 2026-06-03, <https://blog.angular.dev/announcing-angular-v22-c52bb83a4664>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.
- [S4] angular/angular, "Release 22.0.0", <https://github.com/angular/angular/releases/tag/v22.0.0>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S5] angular/angular, "Release 19.0.0", <https://github.com/angular/angular/releases/tag/19.0.0>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S6] angular/angular, "Release 20.0.0", <https://github.com/angular/angular/releases/tag/20.0.0>, read 2026-11-12, MIT, summarized.
- [S7] Angular Team, "Announcing Angular v21", 2025-11-19, <https://blog.angular.dev/announcing-angular-v21-57946c34f14b>, read 2026-11-12, summarized.

id: angular-v22-changes
title: "Angular 22: what changed"
topic: angular
summary: What breaks, new defaults, new stable APIs and migrations in Angular 22.
tool: angular
version: "22"
checked: 2026-11-13
sources: [S1, S2, S3, S4, S5, S6, S7, S8]
